L4: ## Integrated Review
L5: Table 1. Application Information
L6: Application type	BLA
Application number(s)	761369
Priority or standard	Standard
Submit date(s)	10/11/2023
Received date(s)	10/11/2023
PDUFA goal date	10/11/2024
Division/office	Division of Nonmalignant Hematology (DNH)
Review completion date	10/10/2024
Established/proper name	Marstacimab-hncq
(Proposed) proprietary name	HYMPAVZI
Pharmacologic class	Anti-tissue factor pathway inhibitor
Other product name(s)	PF-06741086
Applicant	Pfizer Inc.
Dosage form(s)/formulation(s)	INJECTION
Dosing regimen	
The recommended dosing regimen comprise of a loading dose of 300 mg for the first week followed by once weekly dosing of 150 mg by subcutaneous injection. The recommended dosage of HYMPAVZI for patients 12 years of age and older, weighing at least 35 kg is as follows:
Day 1: Administer 300 mg (two 150 mg injections) by subcutaneous injection (loading dose)
Day 8 and thereafter: Administer 150 mg every week by subcutaneous injection

Applicant-proposed indication(s)/population(s)	
For routine prophylaxis (to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes) in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with:
Hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) without factor VIII inhibitors, or
Hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency) without factor IX inhibitors.

SNOMED CT code for proposed indication disease term(s)1	234442002 (Hereditary factor VIII deficiency disease without inhibitor)
438792009 (Hereditary factor IX deficiency disease without inhibitor)
Regulatory action	Approval
Approved dosage (if applicable)	
The recommended dosage of HYMPAVZI for adult and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older is as follows:
Loading dose
300 mg (two 150 mg subcutaneous injections)
If more than one injection is required to deliver a complete dose, administer each injection at a different injection site.
Maintenance dose
One week after the loading dose, initiate maintenance dosing of 150 mg every week by subcutaneous injection on the same day each week, at any time of day.

L35: ABR	annualized bleeding rate

AE	adverse event

AUC	area under the concentration-time curve
BLA	biologics license application
CL	clearance
Cmax	maximum plasma concentration
DDI	drug-drug interaction
DDIM	D-dimer concentration
dPT	diluted prothrombin time
EC50	half-maximal effective concentration
ECL	electrochemiluminescence
ECG	electrocardiogram
E-R	exposure-response
FDA	Food and Drug Administration
FIB	fibrinogen concentration
FMQ	FDA Medical Query
FIX	factor IX
FVIII	factor VIII
FX	factor X
GMR	geometric mean ratio
GCP	good clinical practice
HA	hemophilia A
Haem-A-QoL	Hematology A Quality of Life
HB	hemophilia B
IND	investigational new drug
IgG1	immunoglobulin G1
ISP	integrated safety population
IU	international units
IV	intravenous
M1	margin 1
M2	margin 2
mAb	monoclonal antibody

NAb	neutralizing antibody
NI	noninferiority
NOAEL	no observed adverse effect level

L39: ## I. Executive Summary
L40: ## 1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L41: On October 11, 2023, Pfizer, Inc, submitted a new BLA for marstacimab for the following indications: HYMPAVZI is a tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI)-directed antibody indicated for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with hemophilia A (HA) (congenital factor VIII deficiency) without factor VIII inhibitors and hemophilia B (HB) (congenital factor IX deficiency) without factor IX inhibitors.
L42: On May 18, 2016, marstacimab received orphan drug designation for treatment of Hemophilia A and Hemophilia B patients with or without inhibitors. Fast Track Designation was granted on September 17, 2019.
L43: The Applicant submitted results from one adequate and well-controlled clinical study (Study B7841005) that served as the equivalent of two studies, which provides substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) of marstacimab for the indication. Study B7841005 is an open-label, multi-center, two phase (observation and active treatment) study of marstacimab in patients aged 12 years and above with severe HA and moderately severe to severe HB with or without inhibitors. Participants were enrolled to independent cohorts based on presence or absence of inhibitor and treatment regimen at the time of enrollment (on-demand or prophylaxis factor replacement). The efficacy of marstacimab was based on the results from the noninhibitor cohort, observational phase on-demand (OP-OD), comprising participants who received factor on demand prior to enrollment and during the 6-month OP and cohort observational phase prophylaxis (OP-PPX), comprising participants who received prophylaxis with factor prior to enrollment and during the 6-month observation period. Following the OP, all participants received 12 months of marstacimab prophylaxis during the active treatment phase (ATP). For each cohort, the study compared treatment with factor-replacement therapy during OP with that of marstacimab prophylaxis treatment during ATP.
L44: Marstacimab prophylaxis demonstrated superiority over on-demand factor replacement (Cohort OP-OD) and noninferiority to prophylaxis with factor (Cohort OP-PPX) based on the annualized bleeding rate (ABR) of targeted bleeds (primary endpoint), spontaneous, joint, total, and target joint bleeds (secondary endpoints), in participants with HA and HB without inhibitor. An ABR endpoint has been used to support approval for products to treat bleeding events due to hemophilia in the past. Prevention of bleeding is considered of clinical benefit, by reducing the morbidity and mortality associated with hemophilia, including joint damage and stroke.
L45: The safety profile of marstacimab prophylaxis was determined to be acceptable for the proposed indication, and toxicities can be addressed in labeling. Enhanced pharmacovigilance is recommended for thromboembolic events based on the mechanism of action of marstacimab and the small size of the clinical studies.
L46: BLA 761369
L47: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L48: This application was reviewed by a multidisciplinary team. Clinical, clinical pharmacology, statistical, nonclinical, and product quality review teams recommended approval of marstacimab in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with hemophilia A or B without inhibitor, and in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with HA without factor and HB without factor IX inhibitors.
L49: The overall benefit-risk is favorable as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework (Table 2) below. For detailed information supporting the basis for this approval, please refer to the detailed reviews included in this interdisciplinary assessment document and the product quality review.
L50: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L51: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L52: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L53: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	Hemophilia A (HA) is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations in the FVIII gene which results in a severe deficiency or absence of the clotting protein factor VIII. HA occurs in approximately 1 in 5000 live male births. Patients are typically diagnosed in infancy. Patients are at increased risk for minor and serious bleeding, including life threatening spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. Disease manifestations include mucosal bleeding, easy bruising, soft tissue and muscle bleeds, hemarthrosis, and excessive bleeding with procedures or trauma. Bleeding can also occur in vital organs (including the brain if left untreated), recurrent bleeding can lead to damage in skeletal muscles and joints, causing progressive and debilitating arthropathy, resulting joint dysfunction, and decreased quality of life. Adults commonly experience hemophilic arthropathy due to recurrent joint bleeds, which is associated with significant disease morbidity.
Hemophilia B (HB) is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations in the FIX gene, which results in a severe deficiency or absence of the clotting protein factor IX. HB occurs in approximately 1 in 30,000 live male births. Deficient factor IX levels result in an increased risk for minor and serious bleeding, including life threatening spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. Disease manifestations of HB are the same as HA described in the text above.
One complication of HA and HB is the development of inhibitors to factors, which is a risk associated with the use of factor replacement. Approximately 30%-35% of patients with HA and 5%-15% of patients with HB develop inhibitors. Patients with inhibitors often experience increased disease morbidity and mortality because the bleeding is more difficult to prevent or treat.
Hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) without inhibitors and hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency) without inhibitors are serious chronic diseases with significant morbidity and mortality due to bleeding.
L54: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current treatment options	The goal of treatment in hemophilia is to prevent bleeding, improve joint function, relieve pain, and to assist the patient in continuing/resuming normal activities of daily living.
Current treatment options for patients with hemophilia A or B without inhibitors include, either episodic IV infusion of the missing factor (FVIII or FIX) in response to symptoms of a bleed ("on-demand") or scheduled prophylactic factor (FVIII or FIX) administration to prevent bleeds from occurring.
Prophylactic treatment in patients with severe hemophilia with replacement factor, VIII or IX, has been proven to reduce the frequency of bleeding events and joint destruction in patients with hemophilia; however, it requires lifelong IV infusion every 1 to 2 weeks resulting in treatment burden, incomplete adherence, and complications associated with indwelling catheters (i.e., infection, thrombosis).
Antibodies or inhibitors develop with exposure to exogenous factor and block the activity of factor VIII or IX. Approximately 30% and 1 to 15% of patients with hemophilia A and B, respectively, will develop neutralizing alloantibodies (inhibitors) against infused FVIII or FIX.
Emicizumab (Hemlibra) is a bispecific factor IXa and factor X-directed antibody which was FDA approved for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients ages newborn and older with HA with or without inhibitor.
The effectiveness of the available therapy for prophylactic treatment of bleeds varies. There is a need for more effective therapy.
Treatment with factor replacement can result in significant treatment burden, including need for IV catheters, risks of infection and thrombosis, and the development of inhibitors
There is no approved nonfactor-based prophylactic treatment for patients with hemophilia B without inhibitors.

Benefit	Pivotal Phase 3 Study B7841005
Study B7841005 is an open-label, multi-center, multi-cohort, two-phase study (observational phase [OP] and active treatment phase [ATP] in adult and pediatric (aged 12 years and older and ≥35 kg) male participants with severe hemophilia A (HA) or severe hemophilia B (HB) with or without FVIII or FIX inhibitors. Participants were enrolled to independent cohorts based on the presence or absence of inhibitor and treatment regimen they were receiving for at least 6 months prior to enrollment, i.e., factor on-demand (OD) or factor prophylaxis (PPX).
The benefit of marstacimab for the treatment of patients with with severe HA or HB without inhibitors was demonstrated in two independent cohorts of Study B7841005.
In the on-demand cohort, prophylactic treatment with weekly marstacimab reduced spontaneous and traumatic bleeding episodes compared to factor on-demand. This was evidenced by a lower rate of bleeding events (annualized) when participants were treated with marstacimab prophylaxis compared to treatment with factor on-demand.
In the prophylaxis cohort, prophylaxis marstacimab was noninferior to treatment with factor prophylaxis for the prevention of bleeding events. This was
L55: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
The efficacy of marstacimab in patients with HA or HB without inhibitors was established based on 116 adult and pediatric participants with HA or HB without inhibitors enrolled in the two noninhibitor cohorts of Study B7841005. During the OP, participants received for 6 months the same factor treatment regimen they were on at the time of enrollment (OD or PPX) and then all participants received marstacimab prophylaxis during the 12 month ATP. The primary endpoint was annualized bleeding rate (ABR) of treated bleeding events.
Cohort OP-OD enrolled a total of 33 participants who received factor on-demand (FVIII or FIX) during 6 month OP followed by 12 month ATP with marstacimab prophylaxis.Superiority of marstacimab prophylaxis over factor on-demand was demonstrated for the primary endpoint with an ABR treated bleeds of 3.18 with 95% CI (2.09, 4.85) during the ATP while receiving marstacimab prophylaxis and an ABR treated bleeds of 38 with 95% CI (31.03, 46.54) during OP receiving factor on-demand. The estimated ABR ratio vs. OD was 0.084 with 95% CI (0.059, 0.119), p<0.0001.
Superiority was also demonstrated for all ABR based key secondary endpoints:ABR spontaneous bleeds (ATP-ABR =2.44 [95% CI: 1.61, 3.69]; OP-ABR =30.93 [95% CI: 24.12, 39.67]; Ratio vs. OD=0.079 [95% CI: 0.054, 0.114], p<0.0001)

These conclusions were based on the primary endpoint of ABR treated bleeds and on the key secondary ABR endpoints of spontaneous bleeds, joint bleeds, total bleeds, target joint bleeds.
The prevention of bleeding is a clinical benefit, reducing the morbidity and mortality associated with hemophilia, including joint damage and stroke.

Cohort OP-PPX enrolled a total of 83 participants who previously received factor (FVIII or FIX) prophylaxis during 6 month OP followed by 12 month ATP with marstacimab prophylaxis.
Noninferiority of marstacimab prophylaxis over factor prophylaxis was demonstrated for the primary endpoint with an ABR treated bleeds of 5.08 with 95% CI (3.40, 6.77) during the ATP while receiving marstacimab prophylaxis and an ABR treated bleeds of 7.85 with 95% CI (5.09, 10.61) during OP receiving factor. L57: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and risk management	
Overall Safety Findings
The review of safety focused Study B7841005 and events that occurred during the ATP while participants were receiving marstacimab. Data from the two cohorts were pooled and the safety population comprised 116 adolescent and adult participants with HA or HB without inhibitors who received at least one dose of open-label marstacimab prophylaxis during the ATP period.
The key safety findings were:There were no fatal adverse events.
Serious adverse events (SAEs) occurred in 6% of participants; however, only one of these SAEs was assessed as related to marstacimab, peripheral swelling of the right calf.
Adverse events leading to permanent discontinuation of marstacimab occurred in only 2 (2%) participants. One participant discontinued treatment due to atypical meningioma, assessed as not related, and another discontinued due to peripheral swelling, assessed as related.
Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were reported in 64% of participants while receiving marstacimab prophylaxis during the ATP. The most common TEAEs, occurring in ≥5% of participants, were viral infection (21%), local administration reactions (10%), hemorrhage (10%), nasopharyngitis (9%), headache (7%) and pruritus (7%). The most frequently reported adverse reaction observed in ≥10% of participants while receiving marstacimab with marstacimab were injection site reactions.

Thromboembolic events are an adverse event of special interest based on the mechanism of action of marstacimab.In Study B7841005, there were no events of thrombosis reported in participant while receiving marstacimab during the ATP.
There was 1 case of a thromboembolic event (pulmonary embolism and DVT) reported in a healthy participant who received marstacimab on a clinical study.

The safety data submitted were sufficient to characterize the toxicity profile of marstacimab. Although all data was uncontrolled, this was acceptable given the rarity of the disease, clear mechanism of action, and experience with other hemophilia drugs.
Overall the marstacimab was well tolerated.
The risks of marstacimab are generally comparable to those of other anti-hemophilic treatments used for prophylactic treatment for patients with hemophilia.
The identified risk of hypersensitivity and the class risk of thromboembolism should be included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI.
Although there were no reports of thromboembolic events in the pivotal study, given the mechanism of action of marstacimab and the limited size of the safety database, the review team recommends enhanced pharmacovigilance for the risk of thromboembolic events.

L60: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L61: BLA 761369, marstacimab, provided substantial evidence of effectiveness from one adequate and well-controlled study with two cohorts that serves as the equivalent of two studies. The benefit-risk profile is favorable, and all disciplines recommend approval. Hemophilia (A or B) are rare and serious diseases with limited treatment options depending upon the subtype. If left untreated, hemophilia will result in significant morbidity and early mortality from uncontrolled bleeding arising spontaneously, following injury, or related to surgery. In general, affected patients start on a life-long treatment program of blood products or recombinant factor concentrates designed to “bypass” the absent clotting factor, but this treatment carries the burden of frequent intravenous (IV) administration, risks of infection and thrombosis, and risk for development of inhibitors which can result in decreased or ineffective treatment. Additional available therapy includes a nonfactor therapy, emicizumab, to prevent severe bleeding for patients with HA with and without inhibitors.
L62: Marstacimab treatment provides a prophylaxis treatment for patients with congenital hemophilia A or B without inhibitors. For the subgroup of congenital HB without inhibitors, this product has the potential to address an unmet medical need because there is no available nonfactor replacement therapy for this subgroup. The nonclinical, clinical, statistical, and clinical pharmacology teams support the approval based on the nonclinical and clinical data packages and did not identify any deficiencies. No important differences of opinion were voiced during the review process.
L63: The clinical data package consists of a single study with two cohorts that served as two adequate and well-controlled studies that demonstrated an effect on ABR, an endpoint that has been used for the approval of other monoclonal antibodies or blood products for the treatment of hemophilia. The prevention of bleeding, a clinical benefit, is expected to reduce the morbidity and mortality associated with hemophilia, including joint damage and stroke.
L64: Study B7851005 is an open-label, multi-center, two phase (observation and active treatment) study of marstacimab in participants aged 12 years and older with severe HA and moderately severe to severe HB, with or without inhibitors. The study was designed to allow comparison of ABRs during the 6-month observation period, during which participants received the same treatment regimen they were receiving at the time of enrollment, on-demand or prophylaxis with factor, with ABRs during the ATP with marstacimab prophylaxis. The two noninhibitor cohorts formed the basis of establishing SEE in this application.
L65: In Cohort OP-OD, the effectiveness of marstacimab was established by demonstrating superiority of ABR during prophylaxis with marstacimab over on-demand treatment with factor. The mean ABR of treated bleeding for marstacimab prophylaxis was 3.18 (95% CI: 2.09, 4.85) bleeds compared to 38 (95% CI: 31.03, 46.54) bleeds for the on-demand factor.
L66: In Cohort OP-PPX, the effectiveness of marstacimab was established by demonstrating noninferiority of ABR during prophylaxis with marstacimab compared to prophylaxis with factor. Mean ABR of treated bleeds for marstacimab prophylaxis was 5.08 (3.4, 6.77) compared to 7.85 (5.09, 10.61) for prophylaxis with factor.
L67: BLA 761369
L68: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L69: The risks of marstacimab can adequately be described in labeling. Injection site reactions, based on experience in clinical studies of marstacimab, and thromboembolic events, based on mechanism of action and risks associated with other drugs in the class, will be included in warnings and precautions of the United States Prescribing Information (USPI).
L70: Because of the limited size of the safety database for marstacimab, enhanced pharmacovigilance will be instituted for thromboembolic events.
L71: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L72: ## 3. Introduction
L73: Marstacimab (proposed proprietary name, HYMPAVZI), a first-in-class, new molecular entity, is a monoclonal antibody (mAb) antagonist of endogenous tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI). TFPI is the primary inhibitor of the extrinsic coagulation cascade. TFPI initially binds to and inhibits the factor Xa active site via its second Kunitz inhibitor domain (K2). Thus, neutralizing the activity of TFPI may serve to enhance the extrinsic pathway and reduce or eliminate the need for replacement factor VIII (FVIII) and factor IX (FIX). Marstacimab acts independently from FVIII and FIX, and thus would not be expected to result in the formation of factor neutralizing antibodies.
L74: The Applicant proposes the following indication: for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with hemophilia A (HA) (congenital factor VIII deficiency) without factor VIII inhibitors, or hemophilia B (HB) (congenital factor IX deficiency) without factor IX inhibitors.
L75: The proposed dosing regimen is a loading dose of 300 mg administered by subcutaneous (SC) injection on Day 1 followed by maintenance dosing starting one week later (Day 8) of 150 mg every week by SC injection. The dose may be adjusted to 300 mg SC injection weekly in patients weighing \ge 50 kg when control of bleeding events is judged to be inadequate by the healthcare provider.
L76: ## Disease Background
L77: Congenital hemophilia is a rare X-linked bleeding disorder caused by deficiencies of FVIII in HA and FIX in HB. Patients with hemophilia can have spontaneous bleeding events and bleeding events to even minor trauma, which can affect critical organs and be fatal. Clinical hallmarks of hemophilia are joint and muscle hemorrhages, easy bruising, and prolonged and potentially fatal hemorrhage after trauma or surgery. The severity of hemophilia is correlated with residual factor levels, mild (5- &lt;40% FVIII or FIX activity), moderate (1 to 5% FVIII or FIX activity) and severe (&lt;1% FVIII or FIX activity) (Srivastava et al. 2013). Approximately a third of cases of hemophilia are severe.
L78: The frequency of HA is approximately 17.1 cases in 100,000 newborn males (Soucie et al. 2020) and approximately 3.3 cases in 100,000 newborn males for HB (Zimmerman and Valentino 2013), with similar estimated incidence in the United States. In HA, the absence of functional FVIII interrupts the coagulation cascade and the ability to form a stable clot in response to trauma. The resultant bleeding complications can be severe and life threatening and can lead to long-term morbidity from joint damage due to recurrent bleeds. In 30 to 35% of patients with HA, a neutralizing antibody, known as a FVIII inhibitor, forms, rendering exogenous and endogenous FVIII nonfunctioning. Patients with FVIII inhibitors are more difficult to treat in response to bleeding events, leading to a higher rate of progressive joint disease from frequent joint bleeds and increased risk of death from uncontrolled bleeding. In HB, bleeding tendency is
L79: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L80: in accordance with severity of factor deficiency, and patients with severe form (FIX &lt;1%), who account for approximately 30 to 45% of persons with HB, usually suffer from recurrent joint, soft-tissue and muscle bleeding. The reported rate of FIX inhibitors is 1 to 15% of all patients with HB (Male et al. 2021).
L81: Patients with HA and HB are typically treated with blood/recombinant factor concentrates which supply the necessary missing clotting factors. The goal of treatment in hemophilia is to prevent or minimize the occurrence of bleeding to improve joint function, alleviate pain, and help the patient to continue/resume regular daily activities. However, up to 32% of patients with severe HA and 5% of patients with severe HB develop neutralizing antibodies to the administered factors rendering the factor concentrates ineffective to prevent bleeding events. The patients who have hemophilia and inhibitors are then treated with a bypassing strategy designed to overcome the missing factor and inhibitor.
L82: Treatment with replacement factor can either be in response to a bleeding event (episodic or “on demand”) or scheduled to prevent bleeds by maintaining factor levels in a protective range (prophylactic treatment). The aim of prophylaxis is to reduce FVIII or FIX deficiencies and reduced spontaneous bleeding events by maintaining factor activity of at least 1% (Berntorp and Shapiro 2012). There is significant evidence that prophylactic treatment in children with severe hemophilia helps prevent bleeding episodes and the associated joint damage that is a major morbidity in patients with hemophilia (Manco-Johnson et al. 2007). In adults with severe hemophilia, an early start of treatment with long-term prophylaxis is reported to reduce bleeding frequency and subsequent chronic joint disease and result in better quality of life (Khawaji et al. 2011). The choice of regimens (i.e., on-demand or prophylaxis with factor) is multi-factorial and may be based on frequency and severity of bleeding, patient preference and lifestyle, and regional/geographic standard of care. In resource-rich countries, prophylactic treatment initiated prior to any joint bleeds is preferred.
L83: Over the past 50 years, the treatment options for hemophilia have advanced. Initial outcomes measures were focused on raising the factor activity levels. With the incorporation of prophylactic therapy, annualized bleeding rate (ABR) became the primary outcome measure (Konkle et al. 2019). Bleeding rates can be impacted by several patient-specific factors as well as external factors, such as clotting factor level, age, overall health, activity level, and seasonality. There can be some subjectivity with measurement of ABR as the patient and treating physician must each define what constitutes a bleeding event; ABR generally only measures recognized bleeding events and may not capture subclinical bleeds. Overall, ABR is a clinically meaningful endpoint as a reduction in bleeding events is important to patients by decreasing risk of mortality, organ and tissue damage, including joint damage, which is associated with recurrent bleeds, and allowing greater activity that might otherwise be limited due to risk of trauma and bleeding.
L84: ## **Current Therapy Options**
L85: The current treatment options for patients with hemophilia A or B without inhibitors include:
L86: - Replacement of missing FVIII or FIX can be used as an episodic intravenous (IV) infusion of the missing factor (FVIII or FIX) in response to symptoms of a bleed “on-demand” or scheduled routine prophylactic FVIII or FIX administration to prevent bleeds from occurring. Prophylactic treatment with replacement factors VIII or IX has proven to reduce the frequency of bleeding events; however, it requires lifelong IV infusion every 1 to 2 weeks resulting in treatment burden, incomplete adherence, and complications associated with indwelling catheters (i.e., infection, thrombosis). There are two type of replacement factors therapy:plasma-derived factors concentrate which have short half 1 to 2 days and require frequent IV injections at based on body-mass-dependent doses.
- recombinant factor concentrates which have longer half-life of 1 to 2 weeks.
- Hemlibra (emicizumab-kxwh) SC injection is a bispecific FIXa-and factor X (FX)-directed antibody, which was approved by FDA on November 16, 2017, for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with HA with inhibitors. Hemlibra was approved for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with HA without FVIII inhibitors in 2018. The recommended loading dose of emicizumab is 3 mg/kg administered by SC injection once weekly for the first 4 weeks, followed by a maintenance dose of 1.5 mg/kg once very week, or 3 mg/kg once every two weeks, or 6 mg/kg once every four weeks. The emicizumab product label contains a boxed warning that states cases of thrombotic microangiopathy and thrombotic events were reported when on average a cumulative amount of &gt;100U/kg/24 hours of activated prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC) was administered for 24 hours or more to patients receiving emicizumab prophylaxis. The boxed warning contains the recommendation to monitor for the development of thrombotic microangiopathy and thrombotic events if aPCC is administered, and to discontinue aPCC and suspend dosing of emicizumab if symptoms occur.
- Gene therapy: Gene therapy achieved by infusion of a recombinant, adeno-associated virus-based vector containing the gene for the missing coagulation factor therapy (i.e., Hemgenix for HB and ROCTAVIAN for HA).
L87: The World Federation of Hemophilia (WFH) strongly recommends prophylactic treatment for patients with hemophilia A or B with a severe phenotype sufficient to prevent bleeds at all times. Prophylaxis should be individualized (adjusting prophylaxis dose/frequency or both), taking into consideration patient bleeding phenotype, joint status, individual pharmacokinetics, and patient self-assessment and preference (Srivastava et al. 2020).
L88: Marstacimab is a first-in-class TFPI antagonist with a mechanism of action that is different than the other available nonfactor product therapy and provides for an alternative nonfactor treatment option for patients with HA and HB without inhibitors. Marstacimab is the first nonfactor replacement therapy that will be available for patients with HB without inhibitors, which addresses an unmet medical need.

L94: ## 3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
L95: The early phase studies, summarized in Section 14, assessed the bioequivalence, pharmacokinetic (PK), and pharmacodynamic (PD) characteristics of different dosing regimen and provided preliminary safety, immunogenicity, and tolerability of marstacimab.
L96: Table 3 provides an overview of the clinical studies conducted to support the benefit-risk assessment of marstacimab for participants with HA and HB without inhibitors. Results from two independent cohorts of participants with HA and HA without inhibitor from the pivotal study, Study 7841005 (Cohort OP-OD and Cohort OP-PPD) formed the basis of the assessment of benefit. The assessment of benefit was based on a comparison of efficacy data from the active treatment phase (ATP), during which all participants received marstacimab, and efficacy data from the OP, during which participants received treatment based on their cohort assignment (factor on-demand factor or factor prophylaxis).
L97: The assessment of risk was based primarily on data derived from Study 7841005 during 12-month ATP period with marstacimab prophylaxis along with analysis of safety data from long-term open-label study (Study B7841007) as well as a pooled safety set comprising all adverse events occurring in participants with HA or HB with or without inhibitor occurred while receiving marstacimab on a clinical study.

L110: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L111: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L112: Marstacimab is an immunoglobulin G1 (IgG1) mAb TFPI antagonist designed to prevent bleeding by enhancing the extrinsic coagulation pathway in patients with hemophilia (A and B). Both conditions are inherited X-linked genetic disorders that occur almost exclusively in males. Only rare instances have been reported in females where both X chromosomes are affected or if one X chromosome is affected and the other X chromosome is absent or nonfunctional.
L113: Patients with HA and HB are susceptible to bleeding due to deficiency in factor VIII and factor IX, respectively (see Figure 1). Deficiencies in coagulation factor IX (HB) or factor VIII (HA) of the intrinsic pathway can prevent thrombin formation causing downstream inhibition of the coagulation cascade in hemophilia patients, which may result in excessive bleeding.
L114: **Figure 1. Intrinsic and Extrinsic Coagulation Pathway**
L115: Source: Modified Applicant figure
L116: Abbreviations: TF: tissue factor; TFPI: tissue factor pathway inhibitor
L117: Tissue factor (TF)1 is an extrinsic coagulation protein released in response to extravascular cell damage that enhances FVII and FX, independent of intrinsic coagulation pathway. Thus, strategies to increase TF activity might enhance coagulation in both HA and HB.
L118: Normally, TF activity is negatively regulated by TFPI2 (shown in Figure 1 in red). The role of TF and TFPI in the extrinsic coagulation pathway is depicted in Figure 2. TFPI is an endogenous serine protease inhibitor containing at least three known Kunitz-type domains (K1, K2 and K3) separated by short stretches of amino acids. Marstacimab binds to TFPI Kunitz 2 (K2) domain, preventing TFPI from inhibiting TF.
L119: 1 TF is an integral membrane bound blood coagulation protein expressed in many cells in organs such as lungs, brain, heart, testes, uterus, and placenta. Vascular tissue injury or disruption in endovascular activity, can trigger TF/FVIIa/FXa complex to promote and activate the extrinsic coagulation pathway, enhancing the conversion of prothrombin to thrombin and consequently formation of blood clot at the site of injury.
L120: 2 TFPI is an endogenous protease inhibitor produced by the endothelial cells of the vascular smooth muscle cells and several blood cells (i.e., megakaryocytes/ platelets, monocyte) that circulates in plasma. There are two known isomers of TFPI (TFPI\alpha and TFPI \beta). TFPI\alpha is the full-length isoform consisting of three Kunitz-type-domains K1, K2, and K3, the latest not bearing inhibitory function. The anticoagulant action of TFPI is proposed to occur in a two-stage process. Release of K2 binds FXa and inactivates it followed by K1 binding to TF-FVIIa complex that prevents further activation of Factor X (Price et al. 2004).
L121: Figure 2. Schematic Presentation of the TF and TFPI and Marstacimab Site of Action
L122: Source: Modified figure from Crawley and Lane (2008)
L123: Abbreviations: FVIIa, factor VIIa; FX, factor X; FXa, factor Xa; TF, tissue factor; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor
L124: As depicted in Figure 2, the extrinsic clotting pathway is normally triggered by TF in response to tissue injury (1), which forms a complex with FVIIa and FX (2), activating (3) and releasing FXa (4) into the surrounding tissue to (5) promote coagulation via the common coagulation pathway, leading to increased thrombin and blood clotting at the site of injury (see Figure 1. Intrinsic and Extrinsic Coagulation Pathway). Plasma TFPI regulates (blocks) the FVIIa/TF/FXa complex (6), inhibiting TF from promoting and activating FX. TFPI binding to the FVIIa/TF/FXa complex occurs via its K2 and K1 domains. Marstacimab binds and inhibits plasma TFPI via the K2 domain (7), which increases the activity of TF and the pro-coagulatory function of FXa.
L125: To establish proof of concept for marstacimab as a potential treatment for HA and HB, a series of in vitro and in vivo pharmacology studies were conducted by the Applicant, as discussed in Sections 5.1.1 to 5.1.3 below. These data demonstrated that marstacimab binds and inhibits the combined K1K2 domains (via K2) of TFPI from humans and nonclinical species. The results of
L126: these studies establish a therapeutic rationale for marstacimab to enhance extrinsic coagulation pathway as a treatment for patients with hemophilia A and B.
L127: ## 5.1.1. In Vitro Characterization of Marstacimab-TFPI Interaction
L128: Marstacimab binds and inhibits human and animal TFPI. Marstacimab has high affinity to human TFPI K2 (K_d = 2.63 \pm 0.08 \text{ nM}) and the combined K1K2 domains (7.9 \pm 0.33 \text{ nM}) but does not bind to the K1 domain of TFPI. In a cross-reactivity study, marstacimab was shown to have a similar inhibitory effect on TFPI K1K2 domains across different species, suggesting their relevance to evaluating the clinical utility (and safety) of marstacimab (Table 5). With no binding to K1, the procoagulant activity of marstacimab is related solely to binding and inhibition of TFPI's K2 and K1K2 domains.
L129: **Table 5. Marstacimab In Vitro Dissociation Constant (K_d) for TFPI K1K2 Domains in Human and Pharmacologically Relevant Animal Species**
L130: Species	TFPI K1K2 Domains, nM
Human	3.7 \pm 0.28^1
Monkey	1.22 \pm 0.03
Mouse	0.58 \pm 0.05
Rabbit	4.25 \pm 0.05
Rat	1.57 \pm 0.04
L131: Source: Reviewer constructed summary table from Surface Plasma Resonance study
L132: 1 Differences in K_d for human K1K2 were considered minimal within 2-fold.
L133: Abbreviations: K1, Kunitz domain 1; K2, Kunitz domain 2; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor
L134: ## 5.1.2. In Vitro Pharmacological Activity of Marstacimab
L135: The pharmacological activity of marstacimab was evaluated in a series of in vitro studies. In a cell-free enzymatic assay, marstacimab dose-dependently inhibited the combined K1K2 domains of TFPI to generate active FX with a half-maximal effective concentration (EC_{50}) of 1312 ng/mL and increased the combined activity of the FXa and FVIIa/TF/FXa with an EC_{50} of 1985 ng/mL.
L136: Marstacimab's ability to enhance clot formation was demonstrated using normal whole blood. Marstacimab significantly reduced clotting time (accelerated clot formation) in whole blood from various donors (Figure 3).
L137: Figure 3. Dose-Dependent Decrease in Clotting Time by Marstacimab Using Whole Blood
L138: Source: Applicant-provided figures
L139: Abbreviation: IgG1, immunoglobulin G1; PF-06741086, marstacimab
L140: The effect of marstacimab on diluted prothrombin time (dPT) and activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) as measures of clotting time was evaluated using human plasma from healthy donors. As expected, marstacimab dose-dependently shortened dPT, a marker of extrinsic and common pathway integrity but not aPTT, a marker of intrinsic and common pathway integrity.
L141: A summary of in vitro pharmacology studies is described in Table 6. Collectively, in vitro studies show marstacimab is effective in inhibiting and neutralizing TFPI, increasing free FXa, reducing clotting time in whole blood, and enhancing thrombin generation in platelet-poor plasma.

L150: ## 5.1.3. Proof of Concept Animal Model Data
L151: The pharmacological activity of marstacimab was evaluated in vivo in mouse models of HA (FVIII-deficient) and HB (FIX-deficient). Marstacimab dose-dependently reduced blood loss in both HA (76%) and HB (65%) mouse models relative to controls. As shown in Figure 4, blood volume loss in the HA mouse model lasted up to 189 hrs (7.9 days) at a maximum dose of 6 mg/kg (a human equivalent dose of 360 mg based on body weight), which is similar to the maximum recommended once weekly human dose of 300 mg. The in vivo studies in mouse models suggest that marstacimab might increase fibrin deposition and increase platelet accumulation in hemophilia patients. A summary of the in vivo model data is shown in Table 7.

L162: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L163: In summary, in vitro and in vivo studies demonstrated marstacimab binds and inhibits K2 domain of the combined K1K2 domains of TFPI (K_d 3.7nM), but not the K1 domain. Marstacimab neutralized TFPI, increased FXa, shortened clotting time, increased thrombin generation in whole blood, and reduced blood loss in mouse models of HA and HB. The greatest effects in animal models of HA and HB were achieved at 6 mg/kg, which approximates a 360 mg marstacimab dose for a 60 kg patient. Collectively these studies support a plausible mechanism of action by which marstacimab may be a potentially effective treatment of HA and HB.

Clearance	Marstacimab is cleared via linear and nonlinear mechanisms. Marstacimab exhibited nonlinear pharmacokinetics due to target-mediated drug disposition (TMDD) upon interaction with its target, i.e., TFPI. Once the target becomes saturated, linear pathway (i.e., catabolism) dominates.

A typical elimination half-life is not provided as marstacimab elimination involves both linear and nonlinear clearance mechanisms.

Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	Body weight was a significant covariate impacting the pharmacokinetics of marstacimab. Marstacimab exposures over the body weight range 35-120 kg show a trend for increase in exposure with decrease in body weight. However, dose adjustment based on body weight is not required.
Age	Based on the population PK analysis, after accounting for the weight difference, there was minimal difference (3.1%) in CL for adolescents (12 to <18 years old) and adults.
Renal impairment	No dedicated study has been conducted to evaluate the impact of renal impairment. As marstacimab is a monoclonal antibody, pharmacokinetics are not expected to be different in patients with renal impairment.
Hepatic impairment	No dedicated study has been conducted to evaluate the impact of hepatic impairment. As marstacimab is a monoclonal antibody, pharmacokinetics are not expected to be different in patients with hepatic impairment.

ADAs against marstacimab were assayed using an ECL bridging immunoassay and NAb were detected using a neutralizing anti-marstacimab competitive ligand binding ECL NAb assay. The ADA and NAb assays were evaluated by Dr. Jee Chung and was found to be acceptable.
Incidence	In the pivotal Phase 3 study (Study B7841005), 23 participants developed ADA (19.8%) and out of 23 ADA-positive participants, 6 participants developed NAb (26%).
Clinical impact	Participants who received marstacimab and developed anti-marstacimab antibodies had reduced marstacimab concentrations, geometric mean decrease in the range of 24% to 50%, compared to those who did not develop anti-marstacimab antibodies through the course of the treatment period. However, there was no identified clinically significant effect of ADAs on pharmacodynamics, safety, or effectiveness of marstacimab.

L176: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L177: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L178: ## Applicant's Proposed Dosing Regimen
L179: The proposed dosing regimen of marstacimab is a loading dose of 300 mg by SC injection on Day 1 followed by maintenance dosage of 150 mg SC injection weekly starting on Day 8. The maintenance dosage can be adjusted to 300 mg SC injection weekly in patients weighing ≥50 kg when control of bleeding events is inadequate.
L180: ## Selection of the Dosing Regimen for the Phase 3 Study
L181: The Applicant's selection of the proposed dosing regimen for the Phase 3 study was based on the results of the Phase 1b/2 dose-finding study (Study B7841002). Study B7841002 was conducted in adult participants with severe hemophilia A or B with and without inhibitors for up to Day 85 (3 months). The study consisted of four marstacimab dose cohorts: (i) 300 mg SC weekly (Cohort 1; N=7), (ii) 300 mg loading dose, followed by 150 mg SC weekly (Cohort 2; N=6), (iii) 450 mg SC weekly (Cohort 3; N=6) in participants without inhibitors, and (iv) 300 mg SC weekly (Cohort 4; N=7) in participants with inhibitors. After multiple doses, the marstacimab exposure (maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) and AUCtau) increased in greater than dose-proportional manner over the range of 150 mg to 450 mg SC weekly. There was no significant difference in marstacimab pharmacokinetic between participants with or without inhibitors receiving marstacimab 300 mg weekly doses. The observed ABR up to Day 85 showed
L182: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L183: statistically significant reduction in all dose cohorts compared to the historic factor on-demand3 as well as pretreatment bleeding rates (mean ABR: 4.20, 1.47, 4.17, 0.72 for Cohort 1, 2, 3 and 4, respectively, while on-study; 27.6 for historic factor on-demand; and 23.0, 14.7, 20.3, 17.3 for Cohort 1, 2, 3 and 4, respectively, during pretreatment). In general, marstacimab doses of 150 mg to 450 mg weekly were well tolerated. Severe (Grade 3) treatment-related injection site reactions were reported in two participants for the 450 mg SC weekly regimen. Based on the results of the Phase 1b/2 study, while both 150 mg and 300 mg weekly doses showed significant ABR reduction and similar safety profile, a loading dose of 300 mg and maintenance dose of 150 mg weekly regimen, with the option to increase to 300 mg if needed for better bleeding control, was selected for further evaluation because of greater safety multiples and smaller injection volume.
L184: ## **Evaluation of the Proposed Dosing Regimen**
L185: The efficacy and safety of marstacimab loading dose of 300 mg and maintenance dose of 150 mg weekly was evaluated in the Phase 3 study (Study B7841005). Dose escalation was also available to 300 mg maintenance dose if a participant's bleeding was considered not well controlled (see subsection below regarding the evaluation of dose adjustment). For participants who received factor on-demand during the observational phase (N=33), marstacimab prophylaxis demonstrated superiority over factor on-demand as measured by ABR of treated bleeds (ABR 8.0 and 3.18 for factor on-demand and marstacimab prophylaxis, respectively). For participants who received factor prophylaxis during the observational phase (N=83), marstacimab prophylaxis demonstrated noninferiority over factor prophylaxis as measured by ABR of treated bleeds (ABR 7.85 and 5.08 for factor prophylaxis and marstacimab treatment, respectively). Refer to Section 6.2 for more details regarding the clinical efficacy of marstacimab. Refer to Section 7 for details regarding safety of marstacimab. Overall, the proposed dosing regimen seems reasonable from an efficacy and safety perspective.
L186: ## **Evaluation of Dosing in Adolescents**
L187: In Study B7841005, adolescents 12 years of age and older with a minimum body weight of 35 kg at the time of signing the informed consent form were also enrolled and received the same dosing regimen as adults. Marstacimab exposures were higher in adolescents compared to adults, likely due to lower body weight, as body weight is a significant covariate for marstacimab exposure (refer to Section 14.5, pharmacometrics review). However, the efficacy and safety data from both adults and adolescents in the study support the proposed dosing regimen. Furthermore, modeling and simulation using a virtual population of adults and adolescents sampled from the NHANES database demonstrated that the simulated exposure (median C_{max,ss}) in the lowest body weight group of 30 to 40 kg (65.2 µg/mL) were still below the highest concentration observed in participants who received marstacimab 450 mg SC weekly (QW) during clinical development (103 µg/mL in Study B7841002) without any apparent worsening of the safety profile (Figure 5). The results cover the majority of the body weight distribution for 12-year-old children
L188: 3 Per the Applicant, a historical on-demand group was constructed using patients who were on on-demand treatment in the following Pfizer studies: ReFacto AF 3082B2-4432 (B1831004), BeneFIX B1821010, and BeneFIX 3090A1-400 (B1821004). The resulting dataset were further filtered to match the key inclusion/exclusion criteria of Study B7841002 based on age and factor activity (18 \le \text{age} \le 65 and factor activity \le 1\%).
L189: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L190: (3rd percentile weight is 29.5 kg for 12-year-old male children based on the CDC weight-for-age chart (CDC 2001)). In addition, based on the 4-month safety data from the ongoing pediatric study (Study B7841008), which enrolled adolescents \ge 12 to &lt;18 years of age with minimum body weight of 25 kg, no additional safety concern was observed for six participants whose body weight ranged from 33 to 61.3 kg. Therefore, the proposed dosing regimen for adolescents 12 years and older, which is same as that of the adults, is acceptable regardless of body weight.

L193: ## Evaluation of the Proposed Dose Adjustment
L194: The Applicant proposed dose increase to marstacimab 300 mg SC weekly in participants weighing \ge 50 kg when control of bleeding events is judged to be inadequate by the health care provider. This was implemented in the Phase 3 study (Study B7841005), once the participants were on stable treatment with marstacimab 150 mg SC weekly for at least 6 months. To evaluate whether the proposed dose adjustment is reasonable, exposure-response (E-R) analysis was conducted by FDA for the primary efficacy endpoint of ABR. The E-R analysis was conducted using LOESS nonparametric regression method with data from Phase 1b/2 study (Study B7841002), Phase 2 open-label extension study (Study B7841003), and Phase 3 study (Study B7841005) for doses 150, 300, and 450 mg. Fourteen participants who had dose escalated during the Phase 3 study were not included due to limited PK samples after dose escalation. However, the demographic and disease characteristics (i.e., age, antidrug antibody (ADA) status, hemophilia type, and body weight) of these 14 participants did not show any meaningful
L195: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L196: differences to those included in the E-R analysis. The E-R analysis show that there is a slight trend for decrease in ABR with increase in exposure (C_{avg}) (Figure 6).
L197: **Figure 6. Exposure-Response Analysis of Marstacimab Between ABR and C_{avg}**
L198: Source: Reviewer's analysis
L199: Abbreviations: ABR, annualized bleeding rate; C_{avg}, average plasma concentration; LD, loading dose
L200: In addition, comparing the ABR before and after the dose adjustment shows numerical improvement in ABR for participants whose dose was increased. Among the participants who had dose increased during the study (N=14), the mean ABR decreased from 3.57 to 2.98 after dose increase in participants who received factor on-demand during the observational phase (N=3). In participants who received factor prophylaxis during the observational phase (N=11), the mean ABR decreased from 14.0 to 3.42 after dose was increased. This was also confirmed based on interim data from open-label long-term extension study, Study B7841007, which showed sustained efficacy following the dose increase to 300 mg (Table 10).
L201: **Table 10. Summary of ABR Before and After Dose Increase**

L206: For the body weight cutoff of 50 kg for dose escalation, the simulated exposure for different body weight groups was evaluated for both adults and adolescents (Figure 5). Based on the simulation, the median C_{max,ss} values are 65.2 and 44.6 µg/mL for 30 to &lt;40 kg and 40 to &lt;50 kg, respectively, at 150 mg weekly dose, and therefore, with a 300 mg dose, the exposure will go beyond the clinical experience (highest concentration observed with 450 mg weekly dose =103 µg/mL in Study B7841002) in most participants weighing &lt;50 kg, as the exposure increases greater than dose-proportionally. For participants weighing 50 kg or more, marstacimab exposures following 300 mg will stay within the clinical experience. Overall, the review team agrees with the proposed dose adjustment to marstacimab 300 mg SC weekly in participants weighing ≥50 kg when control of bleeding events is judged to be inadequate by the health care provider.
L207: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To Demonstrate Efficacy
L208: ## 6.2.1. Study B7841005
L209: ## 6.2.1.1. Study Design
L210: Study B7841005 is an open-label, multi-center, two phase (observation and active treatment) baseline-control study of marstacimab in participants aged 12 years and above with severe HA and moderately severe to severe HB with or without inhibitors. The primary efficacy objective of the study is to evaluate the effectiveness of marstacimab prophylaxis compared to factor on-demand and separately compared to factor prophylaxis in participants with severe HA (FVIII activity &lt;1%) or moderately severe to severe HB (FIX activity ≤2%) with or without inhibitors.
L211: Study B7841005 enrolled participants with and without inhibitors to separate cohorts. The subject of this BLA is participants without inhibitors. Participants without inhibitors were enrolled to two independent cohorts: Cohort OP-OD (observational phase factor on-demand) enrolled participants with HA and HA without inhibitors who were receiving factor OD for at least 6 months prior to screening and Cohort OP-PPX (observational phase factor prophylaxis) enrolled participants who were receiving factor prophylaxis during 6 months prior to enrollment.
L212: The study schema for the noninhibitor cohorts of Study B7841005 is shown in Figure 7. For both Cohort OP-OD and Cohort OP-PPX, participants received factor for a 6-month OP. Participants received on demand (OD) or prophylaxis with FVIII or FIX-replacement therapy for the OP, depending on the therapy being received prior to enrollment. Upon completion of the OP, all participants entered a one-year ATP and receive marstacimab prophylaxis. All participants, regardless of therapy received during the OP, received marstacimab 300 mg SC initial loading dose followed by 150 mg SC QW. Participants who met protocol-specified dose escalation criteria based upon breakthrough bleeding, could have their dosage increased to 300 mg SC QW.
L213: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L214: Figure 7. Schema for Noninhibitor Cohorts, Study B7841005, Cohort OP-OD and Cohort OP-PPX
L215: Source: Clinical Reviewer figure
L216: Cohort OP-OD: Participants who received on-demand (OD) treatment with factor replacement therapy during the observational phase (OP)
L217: Cohort OP-PPX: Participants who received prophylaxis (PPX) treatment with factor replacement therapy during the observational phase (OP)
L218: Abbreviations: FIX, factor IX; FVIII, factor VIII; HA, hemophilia type A; HB, hemophilia type B
L219: The study design allows a comparison of marstacimab prophylaxis to factor OD and factor prophylaxis, where each participant serves as his/her control. Efficacy analysis compares ATP and OP, results for each cohort were independently powered and alpha controlled.
L220: The pooling of participants with HA and HB together within the same cohort is reasonable. In the coagulation cascade, FVIII and FIX form a single enzymatic complex that goes on to activate factor X, and thus deficiency of either component (FVIII or FIX) manifests in similar coagulation deficits. The mechanism of action of marstacimab is independent of which factor is missing.
L221: The observational period (6 months) of treatment with factor replacement (on-demand or prophylaxis) is shorter than the active treatment period (12 months) with marstacimab prophylaxis. Because the bleeding endpoints are based on the annualized bleeding rate with different durations in OP and ATP, each bleed may carry a different weight when considering the change of annual bleeding rate (ABR). A set of sensitivity analyses for evaluation of different durations and also potential seasonal effect is presented for each cohort in Section 6.2.1.5.1.1 and Section 6.2.1.5.2.1.
L222: ## 6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria
L223: Key eligibility criteria are summarized in this section, and the full criteria are available in Section 15.1.
L224: ## Key Eligibility Criteria
L225: - Male age 12 to &lt;75 years inclusive with a minimum body weight of 35 kg.
- Diagnosis of severe hemophilia A or moderately severe to severe hemophilia B (FVIII activity &lt;1% or FIX activity \le2%, respectively) documented by a clinical laboratory prior to enrollment.
- Subject must also meet the following criteria:No detectable or documented history of inhibitors against FVIII or FIX prior to enrollment (baseline of observational phase),
- Subjects receiving routine prophylaxis treatment with FVIII/FIX replacement, have demonstrated at least 80% compliance with scheduled prophylaxis regimen during 6 months prior to enrollment, and willing to continue to receive routine prophylaxis treatment with FVIII/FIX replacement during the observational phase, or
- Subject with on-demand [factor] treatment regimen with \ge6 acute bleeding episodes (spontaneous and/or traumatic) that required coagulation factor infusion during the 6 months period prior to screening and willing to continue to receive on-demand treatment during the observational phase. Surgical bleeding episodes do not apply to this criterion.
- No known planned surgical procedure during the planned study period.
- No abnormal renal or hepatic function
- Patient should have platelet count \ge100,000/uL and hemoglobin level \ge10 g/dL.
L226: It is acceptable to enroll only males in this study because hemophilia is seen almost exclusively in males. There is no basis to expect a different PK, efficacy, or safety profile in female patients, and therefore, approval should not be limited to male patients.
L227: ## 6.2.1.3. Study Endpoints
L228: ## **Primary Endpoint**
L229: The primary efficacy endpoint is the annualized bleeding rate (ABR) of treated bleeding events.
L230: - Cohort OP-OD: The ratio of the mean ABR (marstacimab prophylaxis versus on-demand treatment) based on adult and adolescent participants meeting the entry criteria with prior on-demand therapy.
- Cohort OP-PPX: The mean ABR difference (marstacimab prophylaxis – prior prophylactic treatment) based on adult and adolescent participants meeting the entry criteria with prior prophylaxis therapy.
L231: In patients with hemophilia, an increased occurrence of bleeding events leads to increased morbidity and mortality, and thus, a reduction in bleeding events is clinically meaningful.
L232: By focusing on treated bleeds, this endpoint assessed the effect of treatment on the most serious and clinically meaningful bleeding events.
L233: ## **Key Secondary Endpoints**
L234: - Incidence of spontaneous bleeds – spontaneous bleed is defined as bleeding for no apparent/known reason particularly into the joints, muscles, and soft tissues as opposed to traumatic bleed (i.e., bleeding event occurring for an apparent/known reason). Only treated spontaneous bleeds were counted.
L235: Reduction in spontaneous bleeds, as opposed to bleeds that occur after trauma, is clinically meaningful to patients and prevention or reduction of spontaneous bleeds is one of the goals of modern hemophilia management.
L236: - Incidence of joint bleeds – joint bleed is defined as a bleeding episode characterized by rapid loss of range of motion as compared with baseline that is associated with any combination of the following: pain or an unusual sensation in the joint, palpable swelling, and warmth of the skin over the joint. Only treated joint bleeds were counted.
L237: Repeat bleeding into a joint can be debilitating and over time result in hemophilic arthropathy. Reducing and preventing joint bleeds is beneficial to patients to help prevent the late complication of hemophilic arthropathy.
L238: - Incidence of total bleeds (treated and untreated) – total bleeds include both treated and untreated bleeds. An untreated bleed is considered a “new untreated bleed” if occurring &gt;48 hours after the previous new untreated bleed at the same site and occurring &gt;72 hours after stopping treatment from the original bleed for which treatment was initiated; an untreated bleed occurring at a different site is considered a “new untreated bleed” regardless of the time of the bleed.
L239: Reduction of both treated and untreated bleeds is clinically meaningful to patients as any bleed impacts patients with hemophilia.
L240: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L241: - Incidence of target joint bleeds – target joint bleed is defined as a major joint (e.g., hip, elbow, wrist, shoulder, knee, and ankle) into which repeated bleeds occur (three or more spontaneous bleeds into a single joint within a consecutive 6-month period). To derive the “incidence” of target joint bleeds in each of the OP and ATP, “baseline” target joints were predetermined before the start of the OP. Only treated target joint bleeds were counted.
L242: Once joint damage and inflammation occurs due to hemarthrosis, a joint can develop increased susceptibility to further bleeding, becoming a target joint. Reducing bleeds in target joints is a clinical benefit to prevent further damage and development of hemophilic arthropathy.
L243: - Physical health domain in the Haemo-Qol (&lt; 17 years of age) and the Hematology A Quality of Life (Haem-A-QoL) (\ge17 years of age) at 6 months. The Haem-A-QoL and Haemo-QoL PROs are designed to assess health-related quality of life in patients with hemophilia. There were not enough participants &lt; 17 years of age to analyze the PRO in adolescents. The adult version, Haem-A-QoL, consists of 46 items comprising 10 domains. There are 5 items in the physical health domain assessing painful swelling, joint pain, painful movement, difficulty walking, and needing more time to get ready. Each item is scored on a 5-point Likert scale (1 = “never”, 2 = “rarely”, 3 = “sometimes”, 4 = “often”, and 5 = “all the time”) and utilizes a recall period of the “past 4 weeks.” For reporting purposes, the individual items are combined to scales (e.g., physical health) and the scales were combined to form an overall total score ranging from 0 to 100, with lower scores indicating better physical health status.
- The Haem-A-QoL Physical Health tool has been used in a prior approval in the treatment of hemophilia and included in the United States Prescribing Information (USPI) for that product, although with a different set of items.
L244: ## 6.2.1.4. Statistical Analysis Plan
L245: The statistical analysis plan (SAP) for the study was finalized on January 31, 2023, and the data cutoff date was April 17, 2023. Key information regarding statistical analysis from the clinical study report, protocol, and SAP is summarized below.
L246: ## Analysis Population
L247: The following analyses sets were defined for the statistical analysis.
L248: - Entered analysis set: All participants who sign the informed consent document.
- Enrolled analysis set: All entered participants assigned to a treatment in OP.
- All safety analysis set: For participants with prior prophylaxis in OP, all participants who received at least 1 prophylaxis or on-demand treatment at OP; for participants with on-demand in OP, all participants who completed any of the procedures for Visit 2 (OP baseline). Participants who changed from a noninhibitor to an inhibitor on or before ATP Day -7 testing were excluded from this population.
L249: (4) Marstacimab safety analysis set: All participants who were administered at least one dose of marstacimab during the ATP.

(5) Modified intent-to-treat (mITT) analysis set: All participants who completed OP and received at least one dose of marstacimab in ATP (excluding participants with inhibitors who were treated with factor prophylaxis in the OP). Participants who changed from a noninhibitor to inhibitor on or before ATP Day -7 testing were excluded from mITT. This population was utilized for all efficacy analyses.
L250: In this review, the population used for the primary analysis (defined as mITT above) is referred to as the efficacy analysis set, and included participants who had both OP and ATP data. Because the primary analyses were based on the comparison between OP and ATP, the efficacy analysis set could not be based on all participants with OP data. With only less than 10% of participants who did not have ATP data, the impact of efficacy analysis was minimum.
L251: ## Sample Size Determination
L252: For adequate powering, the Applicant planned a sample size of 25 participants when marstacimab prophylaxis was compared with on-demand treatment to demonstrate superiority and 60 participants when marstacimab prophylaxis was compared with factor prophylaxis for noninferiority.
L253: These sample sizes were derived via simulation under a range of scenarios. In the simulation, bleeding counts were generated from a negative binomial distribution with the mean bleeds over 6 months of 12.5 for factor on-demand. The mean bleeds over 6 months for factor prophylaxis was initially assumed as 2.5 with an added scenario of 2.35 that was obtained as the weighted average of hemophilia A and B ABRs based on the meta-analysis; the weights were based on the prevalence of each hemophilia type. The mean bleeds over 12 months for marstacimab prophylaxis was initially assumed as 4 with two additional scenarios of 2.67 and 3.46, the point estimate and the upper bound of the 1-sided 80% CI from Study B7841002, respectively. The variance was assumed as 6 times the mean for each scenario; the correlation between the two bleeding counts at OP and ATP from the same participant was assumed as 0.2.
L254: The simulation further accounted for different follow-up times due to drop-out or lost-to-follow-up. To simulate 10% discontinuation during the ATP, a uniform (0, 1) random number U_i was generated for each participant; when U_i &lt; 0.1, the duration of treatment was curtailed into 10 \cdot U_i years; the number of bleeding X_i in this reduced duration was newly generated from a negative binomial distribution with the expected value of \mu_i = 10 \cdot U_i \cdot \mu and variance of 6 \cdot \mu_i where \mu represented the mean bleeding for marstacimab prophylaxis for complete follow up. Table 11 below demonstrated that the planned sample sizes maintain adequate power (&gt;90%) for all scenarios except for the most pessimistic case.

L259: 2 Initial assumption 12.5 and 2.5; then 2.35 as the weighted average (80% and 20%) of hemophilia A and B ABRs from an internal meta-analysis.
L260: 3 5,000 simulations per each scenario where bleed counts were generated from a negative binomial distribution; each variance was assumed to be 6 times the mean with correlation coefficient of 0.2 between 2 bleed counts from the same participants. To simulate 10% discontinuation during the ATP, a uniform (0, 1) random number U_i was generated for each participant; when U_i &lt; 0.1, the year of observation was curtailed into 10 \cdot U_i years with the number of bleeding X_i newly generated from a negative binomial distribution with the expected value of \mu_i = 10 \cdot U_i \cdot \mu and variance 6 \cdot \mu_i.
L261: Abbreviations: ABR, annualized bleeding rate; ATP, active treatment phase; CI, confidence interval; N, number of participants; vs., versus
L262: ## Analysis of the Primary Endpoint
L263: The primary efficacy endpoint was the ABR of treated bleeding events, which was derived for each participant using the following formula: ABR = \text{number of bleeds requiring treatments}/(\text{days on treatment period}/365.25). If a participant did not complete a treatment period, the days on treatment ended at the last dosing date+6 days.
L264: ## **Superiority Versus Factor-Based On-Demand Treatment**
L265: A repeated measure negative binomial regression model via generalized estimating equation approach was used with log link function. In the model, the number of bleeds was a response variable, and treatment (marstacimab prophylaxis or factor-based on-demand) was a factor. The time on treatment in log years was an offset variable to account for different duration on treatment. The working correlation was set as unstructured. The superiority criterion of marstacimab prophylaxis over factor-based on-demand is if the 2-sided 95% confidence interval (CI) for the mean ABR ratio (marstacimab prophylaxis/factor-based on-demand) was below the preset threshold of 0.5.
L266: The Applicant proposed the superiority criterion for comparison versus on-demand factor treatment for the primary and key secondary endpoints related to bleeding to be 0.5 in the SAP. The review team considered a threshold of 0.5 acceptable because it is more conservative than a commonly accepted criterion of 1.
L267: ## **Noninferiority Versus Factor Prophylaxis**
L268: A repeated measure negative binomial regression model via generalized estimating equation approach was used with identity link function. In the model, the number of bleeds was a response variable, and duration (in years) and the interaction by treatment (marstacimab prophylaxis or
L269: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L270: factor prophylaxis) and duration were factors. The working correlation was set as unstructured. Treatment difference in the mean ABRs was obtained using a contrast within the interaction term. The noninferiority criterion of marstacimab prophylaxis over factor prophylaxis was if the upper bound of the 2-sided 95% CI for the mean ABR difference (marstacimab prophylaxis – factor prophylaxis) lies below the preset noninferiority (NI) margin 2.5 for primary endpoint. The review team considered this NI margin acceptable. Refer to Section 16.1 regarding the Applicant's derivation of the NI margin 2.5 for the primary endpoint.
L271: ## **Subgroup Analysis for the Primary Endpoint**
L272: Results for the primary endpoint ABR of treated bleeds, when compared with on-demand and factor prophylaxis treatment for participants without inhibitors, were summarized by hemophilia types, age, race, ethnicity, and geographic region, respectively.
L273: ## **Sensitivity/Supplementary Analysis for the Primary Endpoint**
L274: Sensitivity/supplementary analyses of the ABR of treated bleeds were conducted to support the conclusion of the primary analysis with robustness under different handlings of intercurrent events (preventative infusion, dose increase, treatment discontinuation, or potentially missing records) and potential concerns for bias (due to carryover effect or seasonal effect) from the primary analysis.
L275: ## **Impact of Seasonal and Carryover Effects**
L276: There was no washout period in the one-way cross-over design of Study B7841005 and the comparison between the 6-month OP and 12-month ATP resulted in potential seasonal effect on the primary endpoint.
L277: The Applicant conducted the following sensitivity analyses for the primary endpoint (ABR for treated bleeds).
L278: - The main analysis was repeated with the same model for the primary analysis by comparing 6-month OP versus excluding the first month of marstacimab treatment during the ATP.
- The main analysis was repeated with the same model for the primary analysis by comparing 6-month OP versus data only matched the calendar days in ATP.
- The bleed rates were compared descriptively during the early part of the treatment period (Months 1 to 6 in ATP) and the latter part of the treatment period (Months 7 to 12 ATP).
L279: The review team conducted additional sensitivity analyses by comparing the 6-month OP with the second half ATP to evaluate the robustness of the primary analysis results.
L280: ## Handling of Intercurrent Events for the Primary Endpoint
L281: The Applicant performed additional supplementary analyses using the same model for the primary endpoint with following approaches to dealing with intercurrent events and discontinuation.
L282: - To assess the impact of preventative treatment for medical/dental procedures, sport activity or physical therapy (plus 72 hours): include bleeding during preventative infusion.
- To assess the impact of dose increase, the main analysis was repeated with the same model, using the following approaches:Including data after dose increase.
- Imputing the portion after dose increase using the mean ABR calculated before the dose increase to give full weight in the negative binomial regression to the observations from participants with such treatment.
- Tipping point analysis using ABRs calculated combining bleeds during the intervention dose and assuming the bleed rate after the dose increases as multiples of successively larger (&gt;1) factors of the former to determine the tipping point.
- To assess the impact of treatment discontinuation, the main analysis was repeated with the same model, using the following approaches:Imputing the portion after treatment discontinuation using the mean ABR calculated based on bleeds during treatment to give full weight in the negative binomial regression to the observations from those participants.
- Tipping point analysis using ABRs calculated combining bleeds during the treatment and assuming the bleed rate after the treatment discontinuation as multiples of successively larger (&gt;1) factors of the former to determine the tipping point.
L283: Although the Applicant considered dose increase as an intercurrent event, because dose increase was allowed per protocol, the Division did not agree, and sensitivity and tipping point analyses for dose increase are thus not included with the review. An evaluation of the proposed dose adjustment (i.e., increase) is included in Section 6.1.
L284: ## Analysis of the Key Secondary Endpoints
L285: The first four key secondary endpoints of incidence of (1) spontaneous bleeds, (2) joint bleeds, (3) total bleeds either treated or untreated, and (4) target join bleeds were similarly derived as the derivation of the ABR of treated bleeds and were analyzed using the same negative binomial model as that for the primary endpoint.
L286: The estimand for the bleeding-related key secondary endpoints had the same attributes of treatment, population, intercurrent events, and population-level summary as in the primary estimand. The Applicant did not conduct sensitivity or supplemental analysis for the bleeding-related key secondary endpoints.
L287: Another key secondary endpoint was change from baseline in physical health domain in the Haem-A-QoL at 6 months. For reporting purposes, the individual 5 items in the Haem-A-QoL
L288: were combined to domain scales (e.g., physical health) and the domain scales were combined to form an overall total score. The transformed scale score (TSS) on a scale of 0 to 100 was calculated using the following formula and used for the analyses.
L289: ## **Equation 1. Transformed Scale Score Calculation**
L290: TSS = 100 \times \frac{\text{raw score} - \text{minimal possible raw score}}{\text{maximum possible raw score} - \text{minimum possible raw score}}
L291: Source: Study B7841005 SAP v6 dated January 31, 2023
L292: Abbreviations: SAP, statistical analysis plan; TSS, transformed scale score
L293: Each TSS reflects the average response of the valid items when there were at least a minimum required number of valid items. Otherwise, the TSS is set as missing. To compute the TSS for the physical health domain, a minimum of three out of five valid items is required.
L294: The Wilcoxon signed rank test was used to perform the superiority and/or noninferiority comparison. Missing observations were handled via multiple imputation approach under missing at random assumption. Sensitivity tipping point analyses would also be conducted via successively applying pessimistic assumptions for missingness due to withdrawals because of adverse event (AE) or lack of efficacy.
L295: ## **Hierarchical Testing Sequence and Multiple Testing Strategy**
L296: The ordering of statistical testing for Study B7841005, Cohort OP-OD and Cohort OP-PPX proposed in the SAP is shown in Table 12 (column SAP). During the course of the review, the Applicant clarified that they were seeking to establish substantial evidence of effectiveness based on two independent and completed cohorts conducted under a single protocol, B7841005. The Agency agreed with the characterization of the two cohorts as the equivalent of independent substudies, allowing them to be analyzed separately. As a result, individual alphas with 0.05 could be given to each cohort separately. The new hierarchical sequences based on independent analysis of the OP-OD and OP-PPX cohorts, which is the sequence used for this review, is shown in Table 12 (column review).
L297: As seen in the table, the testing of superiority over factor prophylaxis in the OP-PPX cohort was not dependent on the results of noninferiority testing of physical health domain in Haem-A-QoL in the OP-PPX. If noninferiority versus factor prophylaxis was established for all bleeding endpoints, testing for superiority versus factor prophylaxis may be conducted using the same statistical model in a way to control the Type I error rate.

L312: ## 6.2.1.5. Results of Analyses
L313: ## Data Quality and Integrity
L314: Data were provided electronically in standard format with a study data tabulation model and analysis data model. Statistical analysis software programs used to create key efficacy and safety outputs for the study were submitted along with the data of the BLA. The Applicant also provided a clear definition file for datasets and detailed analysis programs for assisting reviews.
L315: ## 6.2.1.5.1. On-Demand Factor Therapy During Observation Period, Cohort OP-OD
L316: ## Patient Disposition
L317: A total of 37 participants with prior on-demand treatment using factor replacement entered the study. A total of 34 (91.9%) participants completed the 6-month OP. Three participants discontinued during the OP, two of them due to protocol deviation and one due to relocation. Among the 34 participants who completed the OP, 33 entered the 12-month ATP, which composed the efficacy analysis set for Cohort OP-OD. No participants discontinued during the ATP.

L337: ## 6.2.1.5.1.1. Superiority of Marstacimab (ATP) Over On-Demand Factor (OP)
L338: ## Efficacy Results of the Primary Endpoint
L339: In Study B7841005, the primary efficacy endpoint was ABR of treated bleeds. The model based ABR was 3.18 for marstacimab prophylaxis during the ATP and 38.00 for on-demand factor during the OP. The ABR ratio is 0.084 (95% CI: 0.059, 0.119) with the p-value&lt;0.0001, showing a statistically significant test result. The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR ratio was less than 0.5, thus meeting the predetermined superiority criterion for marstacimab over on-demand factor. These results are summarized in Table 17.
