L345: ## Subgroup Analyses Results of the Primary Endpoint
L346: Figure 8 shows the subgroup analysis results for the primary endpoint by age and hemophilia type. The model based mean ABR for subgroups with sample sizes less than five were not estimated. Overall, the treatment effect of marstacimab prophylaxis compared to on-demand factor was consistent by hemophilia type and age groups. Of note, HB subgroup had a wide
L347: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L348: confidence interval with sample size N=7, although it showed similar direction compared with the overall group and the HA subgroup. Since the sample sizes within these subgroups were small and the study was not originally powered to draw statistical conclusions for these subgroups, the results should be interpreted with caution.
L349: **Figure 8. Subgroup Analyses: Comparison of the Primary Endpoint (ABR of Treated Bleeds) by Hemophilia Types and Age Groups, Study B7841005, Cohort OP-OD**

L351: Abbreviations: ABR, annualized bleeding rate; CI, confidence interval; N, number of participants in treatment group; OD, on-demand; OP, observational phase
L352: At the request of the Agency, the Applicant conducted additional analyses based on further refined age groups (ages 12 to &lt;18, ages 18 to &lt;35, and ages 35 to &lt;75). The age of 35 represented the median age of the adult population across the groups of on-demand factor and factor prophylaxis during the OP. The results showed a consistent pattern across different subgroups (i.e., age, race, ethnicity, and geographic region). Refer to Section 16.2 for more information.
L353: Subgroup analysis was not conducted for different countries as the 33 participants in the OP-OD cohort were enrolled at over 9 countries, making subgroups too small to yield meaningful analysis. Note that there were no U.S. participants in the on-demand factor cohort.
L354: ## Sensitivity/Supplementary Analyses Results of the Primary Endpoint
L355: To ensure the robustness of the primary analysis results, the following sensitivity/supplementary analyses were conducted.
L356: ## Sensitivity Analysis Results for Evaluation of Carry-Over and Seasonal Effects
L357: The sensitivity analysis results for evaluation of carry-over and seasonal effects for Cohort OP-OD are shown in Table 18.
L358: The study did not include a washout period between the OP with factor and initiation of the ATP with marstacimab. To account for possible carry-over effect, the Applicant compared the ABR rate for the 6-month OP and the ABR rate for the ATP, excluding the first month of treatment with marstacimab. As seen in Table 18, the ABR ratio of marstacimab versus OD using the full ATP period was 0.084 (95% CI: 0.059, 0.119) compared to 0.082 (95% CI: 0.057, 0.118) when the first month of ATP is excluded. These similar estimated ABR ratios versus OD demonstrated there was no carry-over effect from the treatment with factor during the OP to treatment with marstacimab during ATP.
L359: To compare the potential impact of seasonality, the Applicant compared the 6-month OP ABR with an ATP ABR based on the matched calendar days and with an ATP ABR based on the second 6-month period of the ATP. While similar, the matched calendar days and second half ATP are not the same for participants whose treatment duration are not exact 180 days during OP and 365 days during ATP. For both comparisons, as seen in the table, the ATP ABR for a subset of the ATP period is lower than the ATP ABR for the entire ATP period. This results in smaller ratios versus OD. For the calendar matched ATP, the ratio OD is 0.062 (95% CI: 0.043, 0.090) and for the second half ATP, the ratio versus OD is 0.063 (95% CI: 0.041, 0.098) compared to the ratio OD based on the entire ATP of 0.084 (95% CI: 0.059, 0.119). This suggests there may be some seasonality; however, the CIs are overlapping and thus no conclusion can be drawn. Most importantly, the upper bound of the 95% CIs for the ABR ratios for both the full ATP and the calendar matched ATP were less than 0.5, the predetermined superiority criterion for superiority, and thus the sensitivity analysis results supported that the superiority criterion versus on-demand factor for the primary endpoint was met.
L360: The Applicant also conducted analyses for the primary endpoint based on the first and second halves of ATP (Table 19) for completed participants who remained on a dose of 150 mg marstacimab SC QW. The mean ATP ABR based on the first 6 months of the ATP (3.99 [SD =2]) is larger than the ATP ABR based on the second 6 months of the ATP (2.42 [SD =3.024]); however, the medians are nearly identical (2.0 for the first 6 months compared to 2.01 for the second 6 months). This suggests the higher ABR during the first 6 months is due to an extreme value, which is confirmed where the max ABR for the first 6 months was 35.93 compared to 10.15 during the second 6 months.
L361: Overall, the Division concludes that neither lack of washout period nor seasonality changed the conclusion that superiority versus on-demand factor is met for marstacimab prophylaxis.

L371: ## Supplementary Analysis Results for Handling of Intercurrent Events
L372: Intercurrent events included preventative infusion and treatment discontinuation or potentially missing bleeding records.
L373: - *Preventative infusion.* By including bleeding during preventative infusion, the model based ABR ratio was 0.083 (95% CI: 0.058, 0.118), similar to results of the primary analysis.
- *Treatment discontinuation or potentially missing bleeding records.* There was one participant who had a dosing gap (\ge 35 days), during which time no bleed was reported. When data during dose gap was imputed using the mean ABR, the model based ABR ratio was 0.082 (95% CI: 0.058, 0.117), similar to results of the primary analysis.
- The tipping point analysis assumed the bleed rate during the dose gap (\ge 35 days) as multiples of successively larger (&gt;1) factors of the ABRs during the observed portions. The results
L374: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L375: demonstrated that unless the tipping multiplier was 154 or higher, the conclusion regarding superiority over on-demand is robust.
L376: The above supplementary analyses results confirmed the robustness of the Applicant's final efficacy analysis results for data of marstacimab prophylaxis superiority comparison with factor on-demand. Refer to Section 16.3 for a summary table of the supplementary analyses results.
L377: ## **Efficacy Results of Key Secondary Endpoints**
L378: Because the primary efficacy endpoint analysis was statistically significant, the key secondary efficacy endpoints for comparison versus on-demand factor were tested in the prespecified hierarchical order, given any previous endpoint showed statistical significance:
L379: - Incidence of spontaneous bleeds
- Incidence of joint bleeds
- Incidence of total bleeds (treated and untreated)
- Incidence of target joint bleeds
- Physical health domain in Haem-A-QoL at 6 months

L386: ## Spontaneous Bleeds
L387: The model based ABR was 2.44 for marstacimab during the ATP, and 30.93 for on-demand factor during the OP. The ABR ratio was 0.079 (95% CI: 0.054, 0.114) with the p-value&lt;0.0001, showing a statistically significant test result. The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR ratio was less than 0.5 and achieved the predetermined superiority criterion for marstacimab over on-demand factor.
L388: ## Joint Bleeds
L389: The model based ABR was 2.83 for marstacimab during the ATP, and 32.86 for on-demand factor during the OP. The ABR ratio was 0.086 (95% CI: 0.059, 0.125) with the p-value&lt;0.0001, showing a statistically significant test result. The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR ratio was less than 0.5 and achieved the predetermined superiority criterion for marstacimab over on-demand factor.
L390: ## Total Bleeds
L391: The model based ABR was 7.39 for marstacimab during the ATP, and 47.76 for on-demand factor during the OP. The ABR ratio was 0.155 (95% CI: 0.116, 0.207) with the p-value&lt;0.0001, showing a statistically significant test result. The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR ratio was less than 0.5 and achieved the predetermined superiority criterion for marstacimab over on-demand factor.
L392: ## Target Joint Bleeds
L393: The model based ABR was 1.84 for marstacimab during the ATP, and 23.18 for on-demand factor during the OP. The ABR ratio was 0.079 (95% CI: 0.051, 0.124) with the p-value&lt;0.0001, showing a statistically significant test result. The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR ratio was less than 0.5 and achieved the predetermined superiority criterion for marstacimab over on-demand factor.
L394: ## Physical Health Domain in Haem-A-QoL at 6 Months
L395: The model based median score change from baseline in physical health domain in the Haem-A-QoL at 6 months was -12.35 for marstacimab during the ATP, and -1.12 for factor on-demand during the OP. The estimated difference was -10.678 (95% CI: -24.001, 2.645) with the p-value=0.1161. A negative difference indicates an improvement in quality of life related to physical health. However, the estimated difference of -10.678 was associated with 95% CI that crossed 0, with a p-value=0.1161, and thus is not considered statistically significant.
L396: For the Haemo-QoL (adolescents 12 to 17 years) physical health domain score, there were not enough participants (N=2) for any formal statistical analysis. The analyses of change from baseline in physical health domain in Haem-A-QoL (adults \ge17 years) at 6 months was based on 31 out of 33 participants.
L397: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L398: No participant discontinued the study due to lack of efficacy or adverse event; thus, the tipping point analysis of change from baseline at 6 months in physical health domain of the Haem-A-QoL for on-demand treatment at OP was not performed.
L399: At the request of the Agency, the Applicant conducted nonparametric analyses for the primary and key secondary endpoints related to ABR to assess the robustness of the primary analysis results. Their results based on the nonparametric approach (i.e., Wilcoxon signed rank test) for the ABR differences (ATP - OP) showed p-value&lt;0.0001 for all major efficacy endpoints related to ABR. The review team confirmed these results and therefore concluded that the primary analysis results for on-demand superiority comparisons are positive with statistical significance.
L400: ## 6.2.1.5.2. Prophylaxis Factor Therapy During Observation Period, Cohort OP-PPX
L401: ## **Patient Disposition**
L402: A total of 91 participants with prior treatment with factor prophylaxis entered the study. Of those, a total of 84 (92.3%) participants completed the 6-month OP. Seven participants discontinued during the OP, 2 of them due to protocol deviation and 5 due to no longer meeting the eligibility criteria. Among the 84 participants who completed the OP, 83 entered the 12-month ATP treatment with marstacimab. These 83 participants composed the efficacy analysis set for Cohort OP-PPX. During the ATP, five participants discontinued marstacimab, 4 of them withdrew and 1 discontinued due to an AE.

L420: ## 6.2.1.5.2.1. Noninferiority of Marstacimab (ATP) Over Prophylaxis With Factor (OP)
L421: ## **Efficacy Results of the Primary Endpoint**
L422: In Study B7841005, the primary efficacy endpoint was ABR of treated bleeds. The model based ABR was 5.08 for marstacimab during the ATP and 7.85 for prophylaxis with factor during the OP. The ABR difference was -2.77 (95% CI: -5.37, -0.16). The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR difference was less than the prespecified noninferiority margin of 2.5, thus meeting the predetermined noninferiority criterion of marstacimab over prophylaxis with factor. These results are summarized in Table 24. The review team also evaluated the assay sensitivity (i.e., the factor prophylaxis during the OP's performance) to ensure that NI results are interpretable. Detailed examination is shown in Section 16.4.
L423: **Table 24. Results of the Primary Endpoint (ABR of Treated Bleeds), Study B7841005, Cohort OP-PPX**
L424: Parameter	Factor Prophylaxis (OP) (N=83)	Marstacimab Prophylaxis (ATP) (N=83)
ABR, model-based (95% CI)	7.85 (5.09, 10.61)	5.08 (3.40, 6.77)
Difference vs. factor prophylaxis (95% CI)	-2.77 (-5.37, -0.16)

L428: ## Subgroup Analyses Results of the Primary Endpoint
L429: The subgroup analysis results for the primary endpoint by age and hemophilia type are shown in Figure 9. The model based mean ABR for subgroup of adolescents in the HB subgroup with sample size less than five was not estimated. The upper bound of the CI for the ABR difference among HB participants (N=18), HB adults (N=14), and adolescent participants (N=17) exceeded the noninferiority margin 2.5, while the upper bound of the CI for the ABR difference among HA participants did not. This issue is discussed further as a key efficacy issue in Section 6.3.1.

L435: and factor prophylaxis at the OP, to bracket around. In general, the results showed consistent pattern across subgroups by age, race, ethnicity, and geographic region except those for the subgroups: age (ages 12 to &lt;18, N=17) had model based ABR difference -0.34 and 95% CI (-3.23, 2.55) and North America (N=11) had model based ABR difference 0.43 and 95% CI (-2.43, 3.29), where the upper bound of the 95% CI exceeded the noninferiority margin 2.5. This was possibly due to small sample sizes within those subgroups without adequate study power to detect differences within subgroups. Refer to Section 16.2 for more information. L437: Note that there was only one participant enrolled at a U.S. site.
L438: ## **Sensitivity/Supplementary Analyses of the Primary Endpoint**
L439: To ensure the robustness of the primary analysis results, the Applicant conducted the following sensitivity/supplementary analyses.
L440: ## **Sensitivity Analysis for Evaluation of Carry-Over and Seasonal Effects**
L441: The sensitivity analysis results for evaluation of carry-over and seasonal effects for Cohort OP-OD are shown in Table 25. The study did not include a washout period between the OP with factor prophylaxis and initiation of the ATP with marstacimab prophylaxis. To account for possible carry-over effect, the Applicant compared the ABR rate for the 6-month OP and the ABR rate for the ATP, excluding the first month of treatment with marstacimab. As seen in Table 25, the difference versus factor prophylaxis using the full ATP period is -2.77 (95% CI: -5.37, -0.16) compared to -2.56 (95% CI: -5.26, 0.13) when the first month of ATP is excluded. The similarity in difference versus factor prophylaxis with and without the first month of ATP demonstrated there was no carry-over effect from the treatment with factor prophylaxis during the OP to marstacimab prophylaxis during ATP.
L442: To compare the potential impact of seasonality, the Applicant compared the 6-month OP ABR with an ATP ABR based on the matched calendar days and with an ATP ABR based on the second 6-month period of the ATP. While similar, the matched calendar days and second half ATP are not the same for participants whose treatment duration are not exactly 180 days during OP and 365 days during ATP. For both comparisons, as seen in the table, the ATP ABR for a subset of the full ATP is lower than the ATP ABR for the entire ATP period. This results in smaller differences versus factor prophylaxis. For the matched calendar days ATP, the difference versus factor prophylaxis is -4.40 (95% CI: -7.10, -1.70) and for the second half ATP, the difference versus factor prophylaxis is -4.92 (95% CI: -7.69, -2.14) compared to -2.77 (95% CI: -5.37, -0.16) for the full ATP.
L443: This suggests there may be some seasonality; however, the CIs are overlapping and thus no conclusion can be drawn. Importantly, the upper bound of the 95% CIs for the ABR difference for both comparisons were less than the noninferiority margin 2.5. Thus, the sensitivity analysis results supported that primary analysis that the noninferiority criterion versus factor prophylaxis cohort for the primary endpoint was met.
L444: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L445: The Applicant also conducted analyses for the primary endpoint (ABR of treated bleeds) of the first and second half of ATP (Table 26) for completed participants who remained on a dose of 150 mg marstacimab SC QW, comparing factor prophylaxis during the OP and marstacimab prophylaxis during the ATP. Although the mean ABR of participants was lower in the second half of ATP compared with that in the first half of ATP (2.82 versus 3.54, respectively), the median ABR during the first and second half of ATP were similar. This supports that there is no concern in terms of seasonal effect.
L446: Overall, the Division concludes that neither lack of washout period nor seasonality changed the conclusion that noninferiority versus factor prophylaxis is met for marstacimab prophylaxis.

L456: Thus, the results for the primary endpoint (ABR of treated bleeds) for noninferiority versus factor prophylaxis were determined to be robust, and do not appear to be affected by any carry-over and seasonal effects.
L457: ## Supplementary Analysis for Handling of Intercurrent Events
L458: Intercurrent events included preventative infusion and treatment discontinuation or potentially missing bleeding records.
L459: - *Preventative infusion:* By including bleeding during preventative infusion, the model based ABR difference was -2.82 (95% CI: -5.43, -0.20) and compatible with results of the primary analysis.
- *Treatment discontinuation or potentially missing bleeding records:* There were six participants with ATP discontinuation or dosing gap (\ge 35 days) during which time no bleed was reported. When data after treatment discontinuation was imputed using the mean ABR from prior to discontinuation and data during a dosing gap was imputed using the mean ABR from before and after the dosing gap, the model based ABR difference was -3.12 (95% CI: -5.73, -0.51). The tipping point analysis assumed the bleed rate after study discontinuation or during the dose gap (\ge 35 days) as multiples of successively larger (&gt;1) factors of the ABRs during the observed portions. The results demonstrated that unless the tipping multiplier was 4.6 or higher, the conclusion regarding noninferiority over factor prophylaxis is the same. A tipping multiplier of 4.6 is considered high, and therefore, we conclude the results are robust.
L460: Refer to Section 16.3 for a summary table of the supplementary analyses results.
L461: ## Efficacy Results of Key Secondary Endpoints
L462: Since the upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR difference for the primary endpoint was less than the prespecified noninferiority margin 2.5 and met the noninferiority criterion of marstacimab prophylaxis compared with factor prophylaxis, the following key secondary efficacy endpoints for comparison versus factor prophylaxis were tested in the prespecified hierarchical order:
L463: - Incidence of spontaneous bleeds
- Incidence of joint bleeds
- Incidence of total bleeds (treated and untreated)
- Incidence of target joint bleeds
- Physical health domain in Haem-A-QoL at 6 months
L464: The efficacy results for the key secondary endpoints are summarized in Table 27.

L471: ## Spontaneous Bleeds
L472: The model based ABR was 3.78 for marstacimab during the ATP and 5.86 for prophylaxis with factor during the OP. The ABR difference was -2.087 (95% CI: -4.232, 0.060). The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR difference was less than the prespecified noninferiority margin 2.5 and achieved the noninferiority criterion of marstacimab over prophylaxis with factor.
L473: ## Joint Bleeds
L474: The model based ABR was 4.13 for marstacimab prophylaxis during the ATP and 5.66 for prophylaxis with factors during the OP. The ABR difference was -1.527 (95% CI: -3.698, 0.644). The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR difference was less than the prespecified noninferiority margin 2.5 and achieved the noninferiority criterion of marstacimab over prophylaxis with factor.
L475: ## Total Bleeds
L476: The model based ABR was 5.97 for marstacimab prophylaxis during the ATP, and 8.84 for factor prophylaxis during the OP. The ABR difference was -2.868 (95% CI: -5.613, -0.122). The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR difference was less than the prespecified noninferiority margin 2.9 and achieved the noninferiority criterion of marstacimab over prophylaxis with factor.
L477: ## Target Joint Bleeds
L478: The model based ABR was 2.51 for marstacimab prophylaxis during the ATP, and 3.36 for prophylaxis with factor during the OP. The ABR difference was -0.856 (95% CI: -2.413, 0.701). The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR difference was less than the prespecified noninferiority margin 1.2 and achieved the noninferiority criterion of marstacimab over factor prophylaxis.
L479: Thus, the noninferiority criteria were met for all key secondary endpoints related to ABR.
L480: ## **Physical Health Domain in Haem-A-QoL at 6 Months**
L481: The change from baseline at 6 months in physical health domain in Haem-A-QoL could not be tested due to the hierarchical testing rules (see Table 13) as the superiority for the primary endpoint (treated bleeds) versus factor prophylaxis was not robust using different parametric and nonparametric methods and thus could not be established (See Section 16.4). The estimated median difference was -2.2 (95% CI: -9.1, 4.6).
L482: ## **6.2.1.5.2.2. Superiority of Marstacimab (ATP) Over Prophylaxis With Factor (OP)**
L483: Although the primary endpoint for superiority over prophylaxis with factor for the OP-PPX cohort could be tested based on the hierarchical testing sequence that considered the two cohorts independently, the results were not found to be robust. Demonstration of superiority was not necessary for the Agency's ultimate regulatory decision, and the Applicant did not seek a claim of superiority over factor prophylaxis in the proposed USPI.
L484: For completeness, detailed analysis of superiority over factor prophylaxis are presented in Section 16.4.
L485: ## **6.2.1.5.3. Additional Assessments, Cohorts OP-OD and OP-PPX**
L486: ## **Impact of Financial Compensation at One Site**
L487: During the BLA review, the review team noted that participants from OMAN site 1091 received financial compensation. Among the five participants in site 1091, there was one participant enrolled to the OP-OD cohort and four participants enrolled to the OP-PPX. For both superiority versus the OP-OD cohort and noninferiority versus OP-PPX, the review team conducted additional sensitivity analyses by different imputation methods (replacing the ABR value by 0, mean, and median of the remaining participants' ABRs), the results remained statistically significant and supported the primary analysis results.
L488: ## **Applicability of Results to the U.S. Population**
L489: Only one participant from the United States was enrolled in Study B7841005 Cohort OP-PPX and no participants from the United States in Cohort OP-OD. Overall, there were 15 participants enrolled in the North America region (6 participants from Canada, 8 participants from Mexico, and one participant from the United States). The Applicant attributed low enrollment in the United States mainly to early shutdown during the COVID-19 pandemic and later resumption of enrollment in the United States compared to other regions. The Applicant asserts that the results from Study B7841005 are applicable to the U.S. population given the well-known etiology of HA and HB, similarity of treatment paradigm of hemophilia globally, and similarity between the population enrolled to Study B7841005 population and to the U.S. hemophilia population with respect to demographic, disease severity, available therapies, and response to treatment. A
L490: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L491: subgroup analysis based on geographic regions of the efficacy of marstacimab prophylaxis found the ABRs were numerically lower in North America (2.57) and the Middle East (2.98), followed by Europe (4.52), and numerically higher in Asia (7.55).
L492: The clinical team concurs with the Applicant's justification that hemophilia is primarily a congenital disease caused by deficiency in coagulation factor VIII or IX. The current standard treatment based on factor replacement (on-demand or prophylaxis) is consistent across global regions. The World Federation of Hemophilia guidelines for the management of hemophilia do not adjust the guidelines based on geographical location. The high-level subgroup analysis of ABR based on region does not demonstrate a higher ABR in North America which would suggest possible lower efficacy. Therefore, the review team concludes that the results of Study B7841005 are applicable to the U.S. population.
L493: ## 6.2.1.6. Conclusions of Benefit
L494: In Cohort OP-OD analysis of superiority of marstacimab over factor on-demand, the model based ABR ratio for the primary endpoint (treated bleeds) was 0.084 (95% CI: 0.059, 0.119) with the p-value&lt;0.0001, showing a statistically significant test result. The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR ratio was less than 0.5, the preselected superiority criterion. The model based ABR ratios for the first four key secondary endpoints spontaneous bleeds, joint bleeds, total bleeds, and target joint bleeds were 0.079 (95% CI: 0.054, 0.114), 0.086 (95% CI: 0.059, 0.125), 0.155 (95% CI: 0.116, 0.207), 0.079 (95% CI: 0.051, 0.124), with the p-value&lt;0.0001, respectively. Thus, the superiority criteria of marstacimab over factor on-demand for major efficacy endpoints related to ABR were achieved.
L495: In Cohort OP-PPX analysis for noninferiority of marstacimab over factor prophylaxis, the model based ABR difference for the treated bleeds was -2.77 (95% CI: -5.37, -0.16) with upper bound of the 95% CI less than the prespecified noninferiority margin 2.5. The model based ABR differences for the key secondary endpoints spontaneous bleeds, joint bleeds, total bleeds, and target joint bleeds were 2.087 (-95% CI: 4.232, 0.060), -1.527 (95% CI: -3.698, 0.644), -2.868 (95% CI: -5.613, -0.122), -0.856 (95% CI: -2.413, 0.701), respectively. The upper bounds of the CIs for the difference were less than the prespecified noninferiority margins, which were 2.5 for spontaneous bleeds, joint bleeds; 1.2 for target joint bleeds; 2.9 for total bleeds. Thus, the noninferiority criteria of marstacimab over factor prophylaxis for all major efficacy endpoints related to ABR were achieved. The sensitivity and supplemental analyses demonstrated that the final results for the primary efficacy endpoints were robust.
L496: Patients with HA without factors and HB without factors have limited treatment options for the prevention (prophylaxis) and treatment (on-demand or episodic) of bleeds. Greater number of bleeding events leads to increased morbidity and mortality. Marstacimab is a first-in-class human monoclonal antibody that neutralizes TFPI activity and enhances coagulation.
L497: Prophylactic treatment that prevents or reduces bleeding, especially joint bleeds and intracerebral hemorrhage, is important for long-term outcomes, in particular prevention of joint arthropathy, in patients with hemophilia. Factor prophylaxis has risks which include the need for recurrent venous access, and the frequency of infusions can be burdensome to patients. Additionally, there is a risk for the development of inhibitors with factor replacement. Marstacimab provides for a
L498: nonfactor prophylaxis option with a subcutaneous weekly dosing regimen for patients with hemophilia A and B without inhibitors, decreasing treatment burden and eliminating the risk of developing inhibitors to factor. Additionally, marstacimab prophylaxis is superior to on-demand factor therapy for patients who are not on a prophylaxis regimen.
L499: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L500: ## 6.3.1. The ATP ABR Point Estimates and 95% CI in Hemophilia B Compared to Hemophilia A in Cohort OP-PPX
L501: ## Issue
L502: Observed smaller ABR difference between ATP ABR and OD ABR in participants with HB compared to HB for Cohort OP-PPX
L503: ## Background
L504: As seen in Figure 9, in the OP-PPX cohort, the mean ABR difference in participants with HA (N=65) is -3.91 (95% CI: -7.10, -7.73) and in participants with HB (N=18) is 1.35 (95% CI: -1.44, 4.13). Thus, the mean ABR difference and upper bound of the 95% CI in participants with HA are below the noninferiority margin. However, in participants with HB, while the mean ABR difference is below the noninferiority margin, the upper bound of the 95% CI is above the margin.
L505: ## Assessment
L506: In response to the Agency's requests for additional information regarding the observed lower point estimate for the ABR difference for the HB subgroup compared to the HA subgroup, the Applicant provided the following possible explanation:
L507: - The observation could be due to four participants in the HB subgroup with unexpectedly high ABRs during the ATP (ABR &gt;10). These four participants comprise two adolescents and two adults. Both adolescents and one adult dose escalated to 300 mg SC QW during the ATP. All four participants continued into the long-term extension Study B7841007. In looking at this further, the higher mean ABR for participants with HB during the ATP was driven primarily by two participants with ABR of 16.1 (b) (6) and 19.5 (b) (6). Both participants
L508: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L509: received dose escalation to 300 mg QW late in ATP at Day 204 (b) (6) and Day 300 (b) (6).
L510: - Participant (b) (6) is an adult who had an ABR of 16.1 while on marstacimab 150 mg SC QW (204 days) compared to 4.68 while on 300 mg SC QW (156 days) during the ATP, with an overall ABR including both the 150 mg and 300 mg doses of 11.2. Of note, the ABR during the OD on factor prophylaxis was 0.
- Participant (b) (6) is an adolescent who, per the Applicant, had a marked increase in physical activity during the ATP, leading to traumatic joint bleeds (14/18 of his bleeding episodes during the ATP). The ABR for participant (b) (6) while on 150 mg SC QW (300 days) was 19.5 compared to 10.2 while on 300 mg SC QW (72 days). His overall ABR, including both the 150 mg and 300 mg doses, was 17. His ABR decreased to 7.38 after 99 days during the open-label extension period. Of note, his ABR during the OD on factor prophylaxis was 5.8.
L511: Figure 10 shows the mean ABR for participants with HB for the OP, ATP, and during the open-label-extension (OLE), Study B7841007. As seen in the figure, the mean ABR was higher in the ATP compared to the OP; however, during the OLE, the ABR decreased to less than the ABR during the OP. The mean (SD) ABR for participants with HB in Cohort-OP-PPX was 3.26 (3.3) during the OP compared to 4.71 (6.08) during ATP and 2.27 (4.02) in Study B7841007.
L512: Figure 10. Mean ABR by Phase for Participants With HB, Study B7841005, Cohort OP-PPX
L513: Source: Applicant's Response to FDA IR Figure 23a_2_2
L514: Abbreviations: ABR, annualized bleeding rate; HB, hemophilia B; N, number of patients in treatment group; OP, observational phase; SD, standard deviation
L515: In addition to looking at those participants with HB with ABRs during the ATP &gt;10, the review team looked at those participants who had a significantly higher ABR during ATP compared to during OD. There were three participants with HB who had at least five more bleeds (annualized) in the ATP compared to the OP (annualize). Participants (b) (6) are discussed above. Participant (b) (6) is 13-year-old who had 0 bleeds reported during the OD and an ABR of 11 during the ATP. All bleeds were categorized as traumatic and occurred
L516: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L517: between Study Days 54 and 314. Six of the bleeds involved joints and five involved soft tissue or muscle, at varying sites. Compliance with marstacimab was 100%. The Applicant stated that in follow up by the Applicant with the principal investigator, the participant was noted to have become more active during the ATP.
L518: ## **Conclusion**
L519: The review team concludes that despite some observed differences in point estimate of the ABR difference in participants with HA compared to HB, marstacimab prophylaxis demonstrates efficacy in both populations. The information provided by the Applicant for specific participants with HB who had extreme ABRs during the ATP provided plausible reasons why these individuals may have had higher bleeds during ATP when receiving marstacimab compared to during OP when receiving factor prophylaxis. Two participants with poor bleed control on 150 mg marstacimab prophylaxis during the ATP compared to factor prophylaxis during the OP appeared to have markedly improved ABR when the marstacimab dose was increased to 300 mg. One additional participant with HB who had a much higher ABR during ATP than OP was noted as being more active during ATP, potentially contributing to the higher point estimate observed in participants with HB.
L520: Given the overall small sample size of the OP-PPX cohort, subgroups by hemophilia type were small and the study was not powered to draw statistical conclusions on subgroups. For both HA and HB, the point estimate of the ABR difference did not exceed the prespecified noninferiority margin. While the 95% CI for the mean ABR difference for the subgroup of participants with HB was above the noninferiority margin, we note that this margin was selected for use with the entire OP-PPX cohort (HA and HB). Reassurance of the efficacy of marstacimab in participants with HB is further provided by the observation in the OP-OD cohort where the ATP ABR was significantly reduced compared to the OP ABR (ABR =23.49 (95% CI: 19.28, 28.63) versus ATP ABR of 0.86 (95% CI: 0.26, 2.8)). Based on mechanism of action of marstacimab, which is independent of which factor is deficiency (FVIII for HA or FIX for HB), one would not expect a difference in effect between HA and HB. Overall, the review team concludes there is a treatment effect of marstacimab prophylaxis in participants with HA and in participants with HB.
L521: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L522: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L523: Marstacimab binds to human TFPI, specifically at an epitope on the Kunitz-type serine protease inhibitor K2 domain (K2), with affinity in the low- to sub-nanomolar range (Section 13.1) but does not bind the K1 or K3 domains of TFPI. Marstacimab showed similar binding affinity to the combined K1K2 domains (via the K2 domain) of TFPI from human, cynomolgus monkey, rabbit, mouse, and the Wistar Han rat.
L524: Toxicology studies of 3 months duration that utilized high doses of marstacimab in healthy rats (up to 1,000 mg/kg/week, representing &gt;200-times the clinical exposure at the maximum
L525: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L526: recommended weekly human dose (MRHD) and in healthy monkeys (up to 500 mg/kg/week, representing &gt;200-times the clinical exposure at the MRHD, based on the AUC) caused an increase in D-dimer and increased the incidence of microscopic emboli in the lung and injection site, but no significantly adverse thrombi were identified by histopathology. A low incidence of acute minimal lung emboli was observed sporadically at the end of the 6-month rat study at 60 (1F, 1M), 180 (4F, 2M) and 1000 mg/kg (1F, 0M) (versus 0F and 0M with the vehicle control), and across other studies (Table 75). These emboli were of uncertain clinical significance because they (a) lacked a clear dose-response relationship, (b) were observed in the absence of adverse secondary lesions in the lungs (i.e., there was no evidence of any lesions significant enough to damage lung tissues or impact lung function), (c) occurred without any evidence of altered prothrombin time, and (d) were not observed in all studies. This suggests that the pharmacodynamic effect in healthy animals is nearly completely muted, presumably due to intact coagulatory hemostatic regulatory mechanisms. Despite the absence of a clear does-response relationship between marstacimab and lung emboli in healthy animals, a hypothetical risk of clotting (e.g., lung or other emboli) in hemophilia patients given marstacimab cannot be excluded.
L527: A fertility and early embryofetal developmental (FEED) study was conducted in marstacimab treated males mated with treatment naïve females. The study identified no adverse effects at 1000 mg/kg/week (i.e., the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL), which represented exposure &gt;200-times the clinical exposure at the MRHD). Based on results of the FEED study, there is no evidence that marstacimab poses a risk to male fertility or the health of the resulting embryo to the time of implantation. Marstacimab was not tested in reproduction studies in female animals because hemophilia A and B occur almost exclusively in males. In the absence of such data, the use of effective contraceptive measures by female patients with hemophilia should be considered during treatment with marstacimab, and for five half-lives after the last dose.
L528: In summary, despite the absence of a robust signal for emboli in healthy animals treated with marstacimab, the hypothetical risk of emboli in patients with hemophilia cannot be excluded. No adverse impact on male fertility or (male-mediated effect on) early embryonic development was demonstrated.
L529: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L530: Although healthy animals in toxicology studies did not identify any risk, based on marstacimab mode of action, the risk of emboli in hemophilia patients cannot be ruled out. Section 7.6.3 discusses thromboembolic events during clinical studies and the risk of thromboembolic events is included in Warnings and Precautions of the USPI based on class labeling.
L531: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L532: Marstacimab is not currently approved or marketed in the United States or in any country.
L533: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L534: The safety assessment for the intended population of patients with HA or HB without inhibitor is based on data derived from a total of 180 unique participants with HA or HB with or without inhibitors who received at least one dose of marstacimab while enrolled in a clinical study including the Phase 1b/2 B7841002/B7841003 studies, the Phase 3 B7841005 study, and the long-term extension Study B7841007. A description of these studies are provided in Table 3. For details on Study B7841005, see Section 6 and Section 15. None one of these studies included a simultaneous comparator arm.
L535: The primary safety profile for marstacimab is based on safety data from the active treatment phase (ATP) of Study B7841005. As per study design, all participants regardless of treatment received during the OP (OP-OD and OP-PPX) received marstacimab prophylaxis during the ATP. There were 117 participants in these two cohorts who received at least one dose of marstacimab; however, one of these participants is excluded from the data set due to termination of the study site for Good Clinical Practice (GCP) noncompliance. This participant did not experience any AEs. Therefore, the primary safety population (PSP) comprises 116 participants, 33 from the OP-OD cohort and 83 from the OP-PPX cohort.
L536: The safety analysis of the PSP is based on a pooled population of the two cohorts because whether participants received on-demand factor replacement or prophylaxis factor replacement prior to enrollment or during the observation period is not expected to impact the safety profile of marstacimab. Limited safety analyses for the PSP by cohort is provided in Section 17.2. This analysis did not identify any significant differences in safety based on type of factor treatment (on-demand or prophylaxis) received during the OP, and therefore, reporting in the USPI by prior factor therapy (i.e., by cohort) is not indicated.
L537: Based on the similarity of the underlying mechanism of HA and HB and the mechanism of action of marstacimab, Study B7841005 did not enroll participants with HA and HB to separate cohorts. For this same reason, a safety analysis of the PSP by hemophilia type is not expected to be different in these two subpopulations. Thus, the primary safety analysis of the PSP is based on participants with HA and HB. A limited safety analysis by hemophilia subtype is provided in Section 17.2. The analysis did not identify any significant differences in safety based on type hemophilia, and therefore, reporting in the USPI by hemophilia type is not indicated.
L538: An analysis of a pooled marstacimab integrated safety population (ISP) was performed to identify additional, rare safety signals that may become apparent only in a larger safety population. The ISP comprises 179 unique participants with HA or HB with or without inhibitor who received at least one dose of the marstacimab in Studies B7841005/B7841007 (n=151) and B7841002/B7841003 (n=28). Of the 151 participants from Studies B7841005/B7841007, 116 were without inhibitors and 35 with inhibitors. Of the 28 participants from Studies B7841002/B7841003, 19 were without inhibitors and 9 with inhibitors.
L539: Three additional studies were reviewed individually for safety. This included six participants with hemophilia A or B from Phase 1 study, Study B7841010, and 60 healthy participants from Studies B7841009 and B7841001. Review of safety data from these participants did not identify any potential safety signals not identified from analysis of the PSP or the ISP.
L540: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L541: The safety assessment performed on data from the ongoing B7841005 study had a cutoff date of April 17, 2023, for noninhibitors cohorts, and a cutoff date of March 10, 2023, for long term B7841007 study. Studies B7841002, a Phase 1b/2, and B7841003 long term treatment were completed. The 120-day safety update provided additional safety data collected from ongoing clinical studies, including study ongoing B7841005, long-term study B7841007 and pediatric Study B7841008, as of cutoff date of October 9, 2023.
L542: Clinical study data were independently analyzed by the Agency. Clinical data scientists (CDSs) from the Division of Biomedical Informatics generated the safety tables and figures. These include safety tables and figures for participants disposition, exposure data, AEs, and serious adverse events (SAEs). Additional exploration and analysis of AEs were performed by the clinical reviewers using JMP, JMP Clinical, and the Office of Computational Science Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA)-based Adverse Event Diagnostics tool (MAED). The Applicant's translations of verbatim terms to the MedDRA (version 25.1) preferred terms (PTs) for the events reported in Study B7841005 were reviewed and found to be acceptable. The review team did not identify any major data quality or integrity issues that precluded performing a thorough safety review.
L543: Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were analyzed at all levels of the MedDRA hierarchy and using FDA Medical Queries (FMQ) narrow. FMQ (narrow) are a collection of approximately 110 standardized groups, where each group comprises adverse event terms that reflect a specific medical concept. Each FMQ term is composed of one or more PTs. FMQs are not mutually exclusive, that is an individual PT may be counted in more than one FMQ. When reporting FMQ analyses, all FMQs, including overlapping terms, are included in the presentation of the data in this review. In addition, not all PTs are included in an FMQ. To assure that all potential safety signals are identified, analysis of all reported TEAEs not included in at least one FMQ were identified and analyzed. These PTs were generally laboratory assessments or vital sign assessments.
L544: - Review of safety was based upon:
- Clinical Study Report for studies
- Protocol for studies
- Data sets for the populations described above
- Summary of clinical safety
- 120-day safety update
- Patient narratives
- Case report forms
- Case report forms and narratives were provided and reviewed for SAEs, and adverse events of interest (AEIs)
L545: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L546: The exposure for the primary safety population is shown in Table 28. The median duration of exposure was 358 (range 22 to 386) days. Among participants in the PSP, 97% were exposed to marstacimab for 6 months or longer, and 75% were exposed for at least 1 year.

L552: In the ISP, the mean (SD) and median (Min, Max) marstacimab exposure were 447 (188) days and 454 (7, 847), respectively. Among participants in the ISP, 89% were exposed to marstacimab for 6 months or longer, and 78% were exposed for at least 1 year.
L553: The size of the safety database is considered adequate for a comprehensive safety assessment of marstacimab for the proposed indication, patient population, dosage regimen, and duration, given the rarity of hemophilia. Although the analysis is based only single arm data, with no comparator, this is consistent with other drug development programs for hemophilia.
L554: ## 7.6. Safety Results
L555: Overall, marstacimab was well tolerated, with few participants having serious side effects or requiring discontinuation or dose interruption, with a safety profile consistent with other approved anti-hemophilic agents. Safety analysis showed marstacimab to have an acceptable safety profile in both participants who received prior on-demand factor treatment or prophylactic treatment with factors replacement. The design of the study was such that each participant served as their own control during the observation period for efficacy but similar to other development programs for hemophilia, the study design did not allow for randomized controlled safety data.
L556: Key safety findings of adverse event analyses of the ATP in Study B7841005 are:
L557: - There were no fatal adverse events.
- Serious adverse events (SAEs) occurred in 6% of participants. The most common SAE, occurring in \ge 2 participants, was hemorrhage which as considered likely related to underlying disease and unlikely to be related to marstacimab based on its mechanism of action (marstacimab is procoagulant agent) and disease of study.
- Adverse events leading to permanent discontinuation of marstacimab were rare, occurring in 2 (2%) participants. One participant discontinued treatment due to atypical meningioma and the other due to peripheral swelling.
- Dose interruption of marstacimab occurred in 13% of participants. The most common AEs leading to dose interruption, occurring in \ge 2\% of participants, was COVID-19 infection (10%).
- The most common adverse events, based on narrow FMQ, occurring in \ge 5\% of participants, were viral infection (21%), local administration reactions (10%), hemorrhage (10%), nasopharyngitis (9%), headache (7%) and pruritus (7%).
L558: ## 7.6.1. Safety Results, Active Treatment Phase, Study B7841005
L559: ## 7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary, Active Treatment Phase
L560: Table 29 provides an overview of TEAEs, based on frequency analysis of the PSP receiving marstacimab prophylaxis during the active treatment phase for Study B7841005.
L561: During treatment with marstacimab, approximately two-thirds of participants experienced at least one TEAE. Most TEAEs were mild or moderate in severity with only 5% of participants having a severe AE. There were no AEs leading to death during the ATP. SAEs were reported in 7 (6%) participants. Permanent discontinuation due to TEAEs was reported in one participant, due to a need for meningioma. Interruption due to TEAEs was reported for 15 (13%) of participants, with most cases (12/15) due to COVID-19 infection.

L571: There were no deaths reported in the Study.

L582: Analysis by narrow FMQ did not identify any safety signal not captured in the analysis by system organ class (SOC) and PT.
L583: The SAEs of meningioma, tonsillitis, and tympanic membrane perforation were assessed by the investigator to be unrelated to marstacimab. Based on the mechanism of action of marstacimab, the review team concurs with this assessment.
L584: The SAEs (PT level) of traumatic hemorrhage, hemorrhage, and hemarthrosis were considered by the investigator as unrelated to study drug. The review team concurs with this assessment and considered these events related to the underlying disease and unlikely to be related to treatment with marstacimab, based on its procoagulant mechanism of action.
L585: - Participant [REDACTED](b) (6) is a 21-year-old male with HA who experienced the SAE of chest pain. On Day 159 of marstacimab, the participant experienced Grade 1 chest pain, which worsened to Grade 2 on Day 167, and resulted in hospitalization for atypical chest pain, cough, palpitation, and occasional dizziness. Extensive workup (including physical exam, electrocardiogram (ECG), D-dimer and troponin, CT angiography of pulmonary arteries, echocardiography, and MRI of brain with venogram) ruled out any cardiac, pulmonary, or brain etiology. The chest pain resolved 5 days later without intervention. Marstacimab treatment was not interrupted during the events. The Applicant assessed the event as not related to marstacimab treatment or any clinical study procedures. The review team assessed this SAE as unlikely to be related to marstacimab based on negative workup and resolution without study drug interruption.
- Participant [REDACTED](b) (6) is a 31-year-old male with HA, with history of synovectomy 15 months prior to start of marstacimab, who experienced right calf swelling on Day 24 of ATP prophylaxis treatment with marstacimab. The participant was hospitalized for the SAE of peripheral swelling with an initial diagnosis of cellulitis and marstacimab held. A doppler performed two days later showed no evidence of above the knee deep vein thrombosis (DVT). The event was resolved by Day 26. The participant declined restarting treatment with marstacimab and decided to withdraw from the study. The Applicant assessed this event as related to marstacimab. Although the doppler did not show evidence of a DVT, because the study was not performed until two days after hospitalization, the review team concluded it could not rule out DVT and based on marstacimab mechanism of action assessed the event as at least possibly related to marstacimab.
L586: ## 7.6.1.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment Discontinuation and Interruptions
L587: Adverse events leading to permanent treatment discontinuation were uncommon during ATP, occurring in only one participant (0.9%) who was diagnosed with an atypical meningioma and required surgery. Participant (b) (6), described above with right calf swelling, is not included as having discontinued due to AE as the narrative indicated discontinuation of study treatment was participant's choice and the event was not coded by the investigator as leading to discontinuation.
L588: Dose reduction or interruption of marstacimab due to adverse events occurred in 13% of participants. The most common, occurring in \ge 2\% of participants was COVID-19 infection (10%). Drug discontinuation rates were low, supporting the tolerability of marstacimab.
L589: ## 7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
L590: **Table 31** presents TEAEs occurring in \ge 2\% of participants during the ATP treatment with marstacimab, based on PT. Overall, 65% of participants experienced at least one TEAE. The most common TEAEs, occurring in \ge 5 participants were COVID-19 (17%), headache (6%), contusion (4%) and hypertension (4%).

L609: ## Adverse Reactions
L610: TEAEs occurring in \ge 3\% of participants during treatment with marstacimab in the ATP based on FMQ (narrow) (Table 32), were viral infection (21%), local administration reaction (10%), hemorrhage (10%), nasopharyngitis (9%), headache (7%) and pruritus (7%), systemic hypertension (4%), and bacterial infection (3%).
L611: The most common TEAEs based on PT, not subsumed by FMQ (narrow) events, are dental carries (3%) and tonsillitis (3%) (Table 31).
L612: The TEAEs of viral infection, nasopharyngitis, bacterial infection, dental carries, tonsillitis, and hypertension were considered unlikely to be related to treatment with marstacimab based on the mechanism of drug, and therefore were not considered to be adverse reactions.
L613: The most common adverse reactions reported in \ge 3\% of participants and included in Section 6 of the USPI, are injection site reactions (10%), headache (7%), and pruritus (3%). The frequency of pruritus reported in the USPI excludes events reported with the PT injection site pruritus, which are included in the event injection site reaction.
L614: ## 7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings
L615: Routine monitoring of blood and urine, including complete blood count, clinical chemistries, and hepatic and renal evaluations, were obtained regularly at prespecified points throughout the study. This section presents a frequency-based analysis of laboratory assessments.
L616: ## Chemistries
L617: Frequency of abnormal laboratory values for chemistry assessments are shown in Table 33. There were no significant changes in laboratory assessments for total protein, serum albumin, blood urea nitrogen, sodium, calcium, potassium, chloride, alkaline phosphatase, alanine aminotransferase, aspartate aminotransferase, estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR, using Modification of Diet in Renal Disease method for adult participants; eGFR using Ped Schwartz method for adolescent participants), reported in during active treatment with marstacimab.
L618: The majority of abnormal values for chemistry laboratory parameters were below level 2. Abnormal values \ge level 3 were not observed for any analyzed chemistries reported in the table. Abnormal values above level 1 were rare, with the exception of low bicarbonate level 15 to &lt;18 mEq/L was observed in 17% of participants during ATP. The minimum bicarb observed during the ATP across participants was 16.2 mEq/L. When evaluated at the participant level, only two participants had more than a single assessment &lt;18 mEq/L. Based on TEAE reporting, neither of these participants had reasons for these lower bicarbonate values or evidence of sequelae. The review team concluded that the lower bicarbonate values observed in the participants were generally sporadic and not clinically significant.

L686: ## **Adverse Events in Subgroup of Patients Who Receive Dose Escalation to 300 mg QW**
L687: Marstacimab dose escalation from 150 mg SC QW to 300 mg SC QW was allowed after 6 months treatment with marstacimab during ATP Day 180 (Visit 14), if a participant met the protocol-specified dose escalation criteria of weighing at least 50 kg and having two or more spontaneous (atraumatic) bleeds of joint bleeds or significant soft tissue/muscle or other site bleed, treated with infusion(s) of FVIII or FIX over a 6-month period in the absence of a confirmed FVIII or FIX inhibitor.
L688: In Study B7841005, a total of 14 participants had marstacimab dose escalation from 150 mg QW to 300 mg QW. The mean (SD) exposure for the 14 participants who received 300 mg QW marstacimab during ATP was 118.6 days (56.3), and the median exposure was 116.5 days.
L689: In these participants, while receiving marstacimab 300 mg during ATP, there were no TEAEs that led to discontinuation or SAEs. A total of 5 TEAEs were reported in 5 out of the 14 participants (36%) after escalation to the higher dosage of 300 mg QW:
L690: - Two participants experienced a TEAE of arthralgia on Study Day 190 and Day 252.
- One participant experienced a TEAE of joint range of motion reduction on Study Day 183.
- One participant experienced a TEAE of laryngitis on Study Day 205.
- One participant experienced a TEAE of rhinitis on Study Day 191.
L691: Overall, the frequency of TEAEs was 36% in participants when receiving a dosage of 300 mg QW compared to 64% of all participants when receiving 150 mg QW. We note, however, that no
L692: conclusion can be drawn about this observed difference as the study was not designed to compare the safety of two doses, and the number of participants who required dose escalation was small with shorter duration of exposure compared to the duration of exposure in most participants receiving 150 mg QW dosage.
L693: ## 7.6.2. Safety Results, Integrated Safety Population Assessment
L694: To identify potential rare AEs that might be detectable only in a larger dataset, we performed an analysis of the ISP, comprising 180 participants with HA or HB (see Section 7.4) Analysis of the ISP considered only AEs occurring while participants were receiving marstacimab.
L695: There were no deaths in any of the reviewed marstacimab studies.
L696: The 120-day safety update was consistent with the original safety analysis.
L697: ## Thromboembolic Events
L698: There were no thromboembolic events in the ISP. There were no events of thrombotic microangiopathy or disseminated intravascular coagulopathy/consumption coagulopathy in any study in the marstacimab development program. There was one case of a thromboembolic event that occurred in a healthy participant enrolled in a bioequivalence study. This case is discussed in Section 7.6.3).
L699: ## Drug Hypersensitivity
L700: There were no reported cases of anaphylaxis, Stevens Johnson Syndrome, drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms, or toxic epidermal necrolysis in the ISP. There was one TEAE event of urticaria that was incorrectly coded under PT "Anaphylaxis." This event resolved without sequelae after treatment with oral antihistamine and the participant continued treatment.
L701: A total of 11 participants had reports at least an event of the preferred term hypersensitivity (see Drug Hypersensitivity in Section 7.6.3 for more details).
L702: ## Hy's Law
L703: The analysis performed on the ISP did not reveal any cases that met the criteria of Hy's Law.
L704: ## 7.6.3. Adverse Events of Special Interest
L705: The Applicant identified the following adverse events of special interest (AESIs) based mechanism of action, safety profiles of other drugs in same class, experience in early phase clinical studies of marstacimab, events that may require additional monitoring or treatment modification: injection site reactions, thromboembolic events, liver toxicity, and hemorrhage. In addition, COVID-19 was designated as an AESI due to the potential to increase the risk of thrombotic events. Table 39 summarizes the frequency of thromboembolic events, hemorrhage, injection site reactions, hypersensitivity reaction, and hypertension.

L712: ## **Thromboembolic Events**
L713: Thromboembolic events were considered an AESI due the mechanism of action of the drug as a thrombogenic agent and the increased risk of thromboembolic events observed with other anti-hemophilic drugs. Marstacimab has not been studied in patients with a history of previous thromboembolic events (patients with history of thromboembolic events were excluded from marstacimab studies).
L714: During the marstacimab development program clinical studies, one healthy participant, who received marstacimab while participating in the bioequivalence Phase 1 Study B7841009, developed a thromboembolic event of pulmonary embolism and probable deep vein thrombosis of the right posterior tibial vein 9 days after first receiving marstacimab and 29 days after vaccination with the second dose of the AstraZeneca (ChAdOx1-S [recombinant] vaccine) COVID-19 vaccine. The participant discontinued the study due to the event. The event resolved and was considered related to marstacimab.
L715: There were no participants with hemophilia across the development program who reported thromboembolic events during prophylactic treatment with marstacimab. One participant reported a Grade 2 (nonserious) hemorrhoid thrombosed which was considered by the investigator as possibly related to marstacimab treatment; however, the event of hemorrhoid thrombosed was attributed to local inflammation and not related to any thromboembolic event.
L716: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L717: There were no thrombotic microangiopathy events reported in marstacimab development program.
L718: The risk of thromboembolic events is included in Warnings and Precautions of the USPI based on class labeling. Due to the small size of the safety database and limited duration of exposure for a drug that will be administered chronically, the Division recommended enhanced pharmacovigilance for thromboembolic events be implemented at the time of approval.
L719: ## Injection Site Reactions
L720: Injection site reactions were considered an AESI based on the occurrence with drugs in the related classes. In Study B7841005, injection site reaction was reported in 11 (10%) participants during ATP prophylaxis treatment with marstacimab. The majority of injection site reactions were Grade 1 (10 participants). One participant reported 70 events of Grade 1 or 2 AEs associated with injection, mostly as injection site pruritus, erythema, or induration. This participant tested positive for neutralizing antibody (NAb) at Day 120. None of the injection site reactions were serious or led to permanent discontinuation.
L721: Injection site reactions were more frequent 9/26 (35%) in Study B7841002 where participants received higher doses (150, 300, 450 mg) for 3 months. Most events were mild (six participants). Severe injection site reactions were reported in two participants (one injection site pain, one injection site swelling) who received 450 mg SC QW (1.5 mL injection volume).
L722: ## Drug Hypersensitivity
L723: In Study B7841005, hypersensitivity adverse events were reported in eight (6.9%) participants during ATP prophylaxis treatment with marstacimab. None of these were severe or systemic cases of hypersensitivity or anaphylaxis.
L724: In the ISP analysis, three participants in Study B7841005 with hemophilia with inhibitors reported events of stomatitis, rash, and anaphylactic reaction. One participant with HB with inhibitor experienced a SAE of papular skin urticaria over the entire body (except head and neck). The event was considered related to marstacimab, and the drug was discontinued. The diagnosis was confirmed by three skin biopsies performed on three separate occasions, and the participant was treated with systemic corticosteroids. A second participant experienced an adverse event urticaria which was reported by the investigator as “Anaphylaxis” (Reported Term for the Adverse Event [AETERM]). The participant was treated with one tablet (a single dose) of loratadine and resolved without sequelae. The participant continued with their weekly dose of 150 mg marstacimab without further reaction. A third participant experienced an adverse event of stomatitis which was considered related to marstacimab.
L725: Hypersensitivity is included in Warnings and Precautions of the USPI.
L726: ## 7.6.4. QT-IRT Study Review
L727: A thorough QT study was not conducted in humans. There were no clinically important changes in ECGs observed in Study B7841005. No participants experienced postdose QTcF values \ge 480 msec.
L728: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L729: No safety review issues were found.
L730: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L731: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L732: Based on the population PK analysis, no clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of marstacimab were observed based on race (Asian or non-Asian), hemophilia type (A and B), mild renal impairment (eGFR of 60 to 89 mL/min/1.73 m2), and hepatic function as defined by total bilirubin 1\times to \le 1.5\times ULN.
L733: Body weight was identified as a significant covariate impacting the pharmacokinetics of marstacimab, and therefore was included as a structural covariate in the popPK final model (refer to Section 14.5 for details). The popPK results showed that weight accounted for most of the observed differences between adults and adolescents (i.e., the difference in clearance decreased from 28.8% to 3.1% when accounted for body weight difference). Similarly, the difference of clearance values decreased between healthy and hemophilia participants (19.9% to 12.1%) and Asian and non-Asians (34.6% to 31.9%) when adjusted for body weight.
L734: No dedicated studies have been conducted to evaluate the impact of renal or hepatic impairment. As marstacimab is a monoclonal antibody that is expected to be metabolized by catabolic pathway and receptor-mediated clearance, the pharmacokinetic is not expected to be different in participants with renal or hepatic impairment.
L735: ## 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L736: No clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) study was conducted with marstacimab. As marstacimab is a monoclonal antibody, PK-based DDI is unlikely to occur.
L737: In vitro DDI studies were performed with marstacimab and bypass agents such as recombinant Factor VIIa (rFVIIa) and aPCC in plasma of participants with hemophilia using thrombin generation assay (TGA). The studies showed that there was no additive effect when marstacimab was used in combination with rFVIIa. While thrombin level increased when marstacimab was used in combination with aPCC compared to marstacimab alone, it was still within the range of marstacimab used in nonhemophilic plasma, which would have full complement of coagulation
L738: factors. Therefore, bypassing agents can be used in cases of breakthrough bleeds while patients are on marstacimab.
L739: ## 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L740: ## Animal Data
L741: Dedicated juvenile animal studies have not been conducted with marstacimab.
L742: ## 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L743: ## 8.4.1. Animal Data
L744: The following nonclinical information was used in support of the indicated labeling sections. Additional nonclinical details are in Section 13.
L745: **Table 40. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Pregnancy and Lactation**
L746: Labeling Section	Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy	Pregnancy: Reproduction studies have not been conducted in female animals based on target patient population being limited to male subjects. Hemophilia A and B are X-linked inherited blood clotting disorder which occurs predominantly in males and is extremely rare in females. Since the risk to female patients is minimal but not absent, recommendation to include the use of contraception is advisable.
13.1
Carcinogenesis,
Mutagenesis,
Impairment of
Fertility	
The carcinogenicity potential of marstacimab has not been assessed. Based on weight of evidence analysis, marstacimab is highly unlikely to pose a significant cancer risk to humans.
Genotoxicity of marstacimab was not appropriate because marstacimab is a protein and considered devoid of direct genotoxic risk.
In a fertility and early embryofetal development study in marstacimab-treated male rats mated with naïve females, doses up to 1000 mg/kg/week had no effect on any of the reproductive parameters (volume of the testis, total sperm count, sperm motility or sperm morphology). Treated males successfully mated with untreated female rats. There were no findings in conceptuses (number of viable embryos, total resorptions, pre- or post-implantation loss). The highest intravenous dose of 1000 mg/kg/week was considered the NOAEL (>200 times the MRHD AUC exposure).
Female fertility studies were not conducted with marstacimab.
L747: Source: Derived from Applicant's data
L748: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; MRHD, maximum recommended human dose
L749: ## 9. Product Quality
L750: ## Approval
L751: The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality review team has assessed BLA 761369 with respect to chemistry, manufacturing, and controls and has determined that it meets all applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. As such, the Office of Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of this BLA from a quality perspective.
L752: ## 9.1. Device or Combination Product Considerations
L753: Marstacimab is provided as a 150 mg/mL solution for injection in either a single-dose prefilled syringe (PFS) or prefilled pen (PFP) for single use for subcutaneous administration. Each prefilled syringe or pen are designed to deliver 150 mg of marstacimab-hncq in 1 mL of solution. Marstacimab is supplied in carton that contains either one single-dose Type I glass prefilled syringe with a plunger stopper (chlorobutyl elastomer) or one single-dose prefilled pen with glass syringe inside the pen with needle guard. In addition, each carton contains stainless steel 27 gauge, 1/2 inch staked needle with a rigid needle shield (thermoplastic elastomer).
L754: The Center for Devices and Radiological Health was consulted for evaluation of this product and found the product approvable.
L755: ## 10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site and Other Good Clinical Practice Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review
L756: Study B7841005 was conducted in accordance with the principles of the Declaration of Helsinki Council, and International Conference on Harmonization GCP guidelines. An Independent Ethics Committee and/or Institutional Review Board reviewed approved and monitored the study. Signed written informed consent forms by participants or their legally authorized representative were required for study enrollment. A Clinical Site Inspection consult review was completed by Dr. Anthony Orenia in the Office of Scientific Investigations and is summarized below in Section 22. A Financial Disclosure summary review is located in Section 25.
