L759: ## III. Additional Analyses and Information
L760: ## 12. Summary of Regulatory History
L761: Marstacimab is a human monoclonal antibody directed against tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI) that has been developed for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with hemophilia A (HA) (congenital factor VIII (FVIII) deficiency) without factor VIII inhibitors, or hemophilia B (HB) (congenital factor IX (FIX) deficiency) without factor IX inhibitors.
L762: Marstacimab was granted orphan drug designation on May 18, 2016.
L763: On November 18, 2016, FDA received a submission for a new IND application under Section 505(i) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act (IND 126734). Prior to the IND submission, three meetings were held with the Applicant:
L764: - Type B pre-IND meeting was held on August 6, 2015, to discuss the overall nonclinical and clinical development plan for marstacimab,
- Type C Written Responses were issued on May 11, 2016, to discuss the nonclinical development plan, and
- Type C Written Responses were issued on September 20, 2016, to discuss the appropriateness of design of the phase 1b/2 study.
L765: On December 16, 2016, the Applicant was advised that the 30-day, safety review of the application had concluded and that the study may proceed with their proposed clinical investigation for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adults and children with hemophilia A or hemophilia B (with or without inhibitors).
L766: Marstacimab was submitted for fast-track review twice. The first submission on May 15, 2017, was denied due to an inability to determine, based on the preclinical data provided and lack of clinical data, if marstacimab had the potential to meet an unmet medical need in patients with hemophilia who have inhibitors. The second review was submitted on July 19, 2019, for which the Applicant was granted fast track designation for marstacimab for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in hemophilia A with inhibitors or hemophilia B with inhibitors on September 17, 2019.
L767: On September 21, 2017, a Type C guidance meeting was held to discuss the proposed clinical program to support initial registration of marstacimab for prophylaxis use in patients with hemophilia A and hemophilia B with inhibitors, and a subsequent submission to support prophylaxis use in patients with hemophilia A and hemophilia B patients without inhibitors.
L768: On February 14, 2018, the Applicant requested a Type C meeting to discuss the quality and clinical proposed drug product development strategy for the commercial drug product (DP) presentations, including both prefilled syringe (PFS) and prefilled pen (PFP). This meeting request was subsequently withdrawn by the Applicant after receiving the FDA's preliminary
L769: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L770: comments on May 23, 2018, stating that the Applicant planned to submit a Type C (Written Response Only) meeting request to gain further clarification in writing, relative to the FDA's preliminary comments and the acceptability of the Applicant's approach to implement Agency guidance.
L771: On August 2, 2018, a Type C guidance meeting was held to seek agreement on key outstanding clinical development items from the September 2017, Type C meeting and to inform plans for a subsequent Type C meeting planned with FDA for October 2018 that would seek Agency endorsement of the full draft phase 3 pivotal study protocol. The FDA advised the Applicant during this meeting that there were concerns with the following:
L772: - The proposal to switch patients without inhibitors who were on stable prophylactic regimens to marstacimab.
- The proposed number of subjects with HB was the minimum number of subjects needed to potentially support an indication and was likely not sufficient to adequately assess the safety and efficacy, given the potential for patient discontinuations and dropouts.
- The phase 3 protocol should specify the number of adolescent subjects (ages 12 to 18) to be enrolled and that a sufficient number of adolescent subjects would need to be enrolled to support an indication.
L773: On August 15, 2018, written responses were issued to the Applicant to provide further clarification on the Type C meeting preliminary comments sent by the FDA on May 23, 2018, in regard to the quality and clinical proposals on the drug product development strategy for the commercial drug product (DP) presentations, including both PFS and PFP. The FDA informed the Applicant that their approach to product quality testing in PFS and PFP was not appropriate because they provided insufficient description of the planned characterization studies and testing plan. Further, the Applicant was informed that they needed to submit the Human Factors Validation study protocol for the 150 mg PFP presentation for Agency to review.
L774: On October 23, 2018, a Type C guidance meeting was held to discuss the proposed Phase 3 pivotal study design, within the context of the described clinical development plan, in follow-up to the August 2, 2018, Type C meeting. The Applicant was informed during this meeting that the FDA had concerns with switching patients without inhibitors who are on stable prophylactic regimens to marstacimab.
L775: On October 29, 2018, final written responses were issued to the Applicant to reach agreement with the FDA on key aspects of the quality and nonclinical development strategy. A key agreement made during this meeting was that the carcinogenic risk of marstacimab appeared low and carcinogenicity studies were not warranted at that time. However, it was recommended that the carcinogenicity assessment be submitted with the proposed BLA.
L776: On March 5, 2020, a Type C guidance meeting was held to discuss the additional data provided from the completion of the phase 2 study, Study B7841002, and the interim analysis of the 12-month phase 2 extension study, Study B7841003 and whether or not the findings were sufficient to allow the inclusion of hemophilia A or B patients previously on stable factor prophylaxis therapy into the phase 3 study, B7841005. The FDA agreed that patients previously on stable
L777: BLA 761369
L778: HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L779: prophylaxis could be included on the study but recommended that the Applicant submit the phase 3 protocol for review.
L780: On December 6, 2021, a Type C guidance meeting was held to discuss the proposed statistical analysis plan and BLA submission strategy. The FDA expressed concerns with submitting only interim data analysis including but not limited to separate BLAs.
L781: On May 9, 2022, the Applicant submitted a Type C meeting request to discuss the aspects of a planned BLA submission. This meeting request was cancelled by the Applicant after receiving the FDA's preliminary comments on July 20, 2022, stating the responses provided by the FDA were thorough and no further follow up was needed.
L782: On November 1, 2022, Written Responses were issued to the Applicant to discuss chemistry, manufacturing, and controls specific questions in preparation for the BLA submission for licensure of marstacimab.
L783: On June 21, 2023, a Type B, pre-BLA meeting request was held to discuss the proposed submission of a BLA for marstacimab. The Applicant was informed that it was unclear if the clinical package would be sufficient to support the submission of a BLA as the Applicant had not described a plan to demonstrate substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE).
L784: On September 5, 2023, the Applicant submitted a request for Breakthrough Therapy Designation Request Advice for the marstacimab for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adults and pediatric patients 6 months of age and older with: hemophilia A (congenital FVIII deficiency) with or without FVIII inhibitors, or hemophilia B (congenital FIX deficiency) with or without FIX inhibitors. The FDA informed the Applicant that there was insufficient information includes with the request for advice to determine if it was appropriate for the Applicant to submit breakthrough therapy designation request.
L785: On October 5, 2024, the Applicant submitted a Breakthrough Therapy Designation Request for marstacimab for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with hemophilia A (congenital FVIII deficiency) without FVIII inhibitors, or hemophilia B (congenital FIX deficiency), without FIX inhibitors. The request was denied due to lack of clinical evidence indicating that marstacimab prophylaxis may demonstrate substantial improvements over existing therapies on one or more clinically significant endpoints.
L786: FDA received BLA 761369 under Section 351(a) of the Public Health Service Act on October 11, 2023. The Applicant requested priority review. The application was accepted with no filing review issues. The application was set to be reviewed under a standard review timeline. The goal date for this application is October 11, 2024.
L787: On April 3, 2024, the proprietary name Hympavzi was conditionally approved for marstacimab after a January 4, 2024, denial of the proposed name [REDACTED] (b) (4) was denied because it may be confused with currently marketed [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L788: ## 13. Pharmacology Toxicology
L789: ## 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With the Investigational New Drug Application
L790: ## 13.1.1. Pharmacology (Primary and Secondary)
L791: Tissue factor (TF) is a procoagulant protein located outside the vasculature that is released upon tissue injury to prevent excessive hemorrhage. Tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI) is a plasma serine protease inhibitor of TF. Marstacimab (PF-06741086) is a fully humanized immunoglobulin G1 (IgG1) monoclonal antibody that targets the K2 domain of TFPI and reduces TFPI activity (see Figure 12). By blocking TFPI, marstacimab enhances TF activity and promotes the extrinsic coagulation process in participants with hemophilia A and B (i.e., those with genetic deficiency in the intrinsic coagulation pathway, in response to damage to the endothelial wall of blood vessels). Given that TF and TFPI are released outside the vasculature in response to injury, they are considered part of the extrinsic coagulation pathway. Both the extrinsic and intrinsic pathways converge on a common pathway beginning with the activation of factor X.
L792: **Figure 12.** Schematic Pathway in the Coagulation Cascade and the Role of Marstacimab in the Extrinsic Pathway
L793: Source: Modified figure of the Applicant and Crawley and Lane (2008)
L794: Abbreviations: FVIIa, factor VIIa; FXa, factor Xa; K1, Kunitz domain 1; K2, Kunitz domain 2; K3, Kunitz domain 3; TF, tissue factor; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor
L795: Inhibition and neutralization of TFPI in the extrinsic pathway by marstacimab is thought to reduce the negative feedback on FVIIa/TF/Factor X (FXa) complex, promoting free FXa to enter the common coagulation pathway to promote clot formation. By enhancing free FXa in a manner independent of the intrinsic pathway, marstacimab can provide an alternative treatment option for patients with both HA (FVIII deficiency) and HB (FIX deficiency).
L796: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L797: Marstacimab binds to TFPI at an epitope on the Kunitz-type domain K2 with similar affinity to the combined K1K2 domains (via K2), with a Kd of 2.63 and 3.7nM, respectively (Study #072904). Marstacimab has similar binding affinities to human, monkey, rabbit, rat, and mouse combined K1K2 domains (Table 41). Binding affinity of marstacimab to TFPI K2 of the combined K1K2 domains relative to concizumab). The triple Fc mutations caused marstacimab to have similar or lower binding to Fc\gammaR than an isotype control IgG1 monoclonal antibody (mAb) lacking effector function, suggesting that marstacimab also has negligible effector function (Study #15GR003).
L798: TFPI is an endogenous serine protease inhibitor with at least three known Kunitz-type domains (K1, K2 and K3) separated by a short stretch of amino acids. TFPI is expressed by endothelial cells in \alpha and \beta isoforms generated by alternative splicing of its pre-messenger RNA (Huntington and Baglin 2018). Mechanistic studies show marstacimab binds to the K2 domain of TFPI (Study #072904 &amp; 121926). The enhanced coagulatory activity of marstacimab is based on binding to TFPI K2. By binding to TFPI K2, marstacimab prevents inactivation of both FXa and FVIIa, enhancing the extrinsic coagulation pathway, promoting hemostasis, and minimizing the need for FVIII or FIX. A comparative study of marstacimab to concizumab showed that concizumab is a nearly 7-fold more potent inhibitor of TFPI K2 in humans than concizumab. The binding affinity of marstacimab and concizumab to the combined K1K2 domains of TFPI is shown in Table 41. Based on binding affinity, the Applicant predicted a clinically effective dose of between 100 to 300 mg marstacimab.
L799: **Table 41. Binding Affinity of Marstacimab to TFPI K2 of the Combined K1K2 Domains Relative to Concizumab**
L800: Product	Human K1K2	Rabbit K1K2
Marstacimab, nM	3.7	4.25
Concizumab, nM	0.493	1.81
L801: Source: Derived from Applicant-provided data
L802: Abbreviations: K1. Kunitz domain 1; K2, Kunitz domain 2; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor
L803: In vitro studies show marstacimab can neutralize the K2 (half-maximal effective concentration [EC50] of 1312 ng/mL or 8.75nM, marked by changes in FXa), and the combined TFPI K1K2 (EC50=1985 ng/mL or 13.23nM) domains of TFPI in a dose-dependent manner relative to isotype control mAb (Figure 13).
L804: Figure 13. Marstacimab Dose-Dependent Inhibition of TFPI Activity on FXa and FVIIa/TF/FXa
L805: Source: Applicant Study #085202
L806: Abbreviations: FVIIa, factor VIIa; FX, factor X; FXa, factor Xa; IgG1, immunoglobulin G1; TF, tissue factor; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor
L807: Marstacimab was effective in neutralizing TFPI activity in vitro in normal whole human blood, suggesting that it is likely to be effective in inhibiting TFPI (platelets and plasma) under normal coagulation conditions. There was a marstacimab-associated dose-dependent decrease in clotting time and clot formation time relative to an isotype control IgG1 mAb. The clot strength was not affected. The greatest activity was achieved at concentrations \ge 16 \mug/mL marstacimab (Study #142056).
L808: Platelet-poor plasma was generated by addition of citrate to plasma from healthy human volunteers, rabbits, and cynomolgus monkeys (Study #123404). Addition of marstacimab to nonhemophilic platelet-poor human plasma resulted in a dose-dependent decrease in diluted prothrombin time (dPT) (EC_{50}=406 ng/mL). There was no change in dPT in the isotype control group (IgG1 or phosphate buffered saline). Neither marstacimab nor the control treatment had any effect on activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) (Figure 14). A dose-dependent decrease in dPT clotting time was also observed in platelet-poor plasma of rabbits (EC_{50}=629 ng/mL) and monkeys (EC_{50}=273 ng/mL).
L809: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection

L811: Source: Applicant Study #123404
L812: Abbreviations: aPTT, activated partial thromboplastin time; dPT, diluted prothrombin time; IgG1, immunoglobulin G1; PBS, phosphate-buffered saline
L813: In the same study, marstacimab increased generation of thrombin time in platelet-poor plasma from nonhemophilic volunteers (Figure 15). These studies demonstrated that marstacimab has the potential to enhance coagulation pathway in patients with hemophilia.
L814: **Figure 15.** Marstacimab Produced a Dose-Dependent Increase in Thrombin Generation in Nonhemophilic Platelet-Poor Human Plasma
L815: Source: Applicant Study #123404

L817: The effect of marstacimab on hemostasis markers (dPT, thrombin generation assay [TGA] and aPTT) was also tested in plasma samples collected from patients with HA (citrate platelet-poor plasma) and HB (Study #135025) with poor coagulation factor activity. Marstacimab dose-dependently decreased dPT clotting times in all hemophilia plasma samples (Figure 16 and Figure 17).
L818: Figure 16. Dose-Dependent Marstacimab-Associated Changes in dPT (Seconds) in Platelet-Poor Plasma Samples Collected From Patients With Hemophilia A

Abbreviation: dPT, diluted prothrombin time
L820: Figure 17. Dose-Dependent Marstacimab Associated Changes in dPT in Hemophilia A With Inhibitor and Hemophilia B Plasma
L821: Consistent with the intended pharmacology, there was minimal change in aPTT (i.e., no change in this measure of the intrinsic pathway). Marstacimab restored thrombin in citrated platelet-poor HA with inhibitor or HB plasma (Figure 18). The findings suggest marstacimab may be capable of restoring thrombin in both hemophilia A and B patients. Marstacimab was also effective in generating thrombin compared to recombinant FVIIa (Eptacog alpha up to 20 µg/mL) and a combination (marstacimab+rFVII).

L825: Marstacimab alone or in combination with recombinant FVIIa (NovoSeven RT, eptacog alfa) produced comparable peak elevations in thrombin levels in platelet poor plasma from HA and HB donors (Table 42).
L826: A marstacimab concentration of 16 \mug/mL produced peak thrombin levels comparable to levels to nonhemophilic plasma, which suggests that marstacimab may improve thrombin levels in patients with hemophilia A and B.

L833: ## **Marstacimab Activity In Vivo**
L834: The effectiveness of marstacimab was tested using in vivo models of HA (factor VIII deficient mice) and HB (Factor IX deficient mice). A laser induced injury was used to cremaster arteriolar endothelium to initiate injury (Study #135244 and #121321). The pharmacological effects of marstacimab on bleeding parameters (e.g., platelets, fibrin formation) were also assessed following laser injury in HA mice. Marstacimab decreased blood loss in both HA (76%) and HA (65%) mouse models. There were dose-dependent increases in coagulation in both HA and HB mouse models. Marstacimab increased platelet accumulation and fibrin generation in precapillary arterioles in HA mice. Marstacimab administered after onset of bleeding restored hemostasis within 2 minutes in the HB mouse tail clip injury model. Marstacimab's effect on reducing blood loss in this model lasted up to 192 hours (Figure 4).

L836: Pharmacological activity of marstacimab was examined in hemophilia A and B mouse models. Marstacimab was effective in enhancing coagulation and bleeding in these models, supporting the proposed prophylactic benefits of marstacimab. 15GR003
In vitro human C1q and FcR binding assays
Method: PF-06741086 binding was tested by C1q binding ELISA and an FcR binding assay (CD16, CD32, and CD64) using ForteBio Octet RED's Bio-Layer Interferometry (BLI).
Study was designed to test PF-06741086 binding potential for complement dependent cytotoxicity and antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity activity.
Control isotype IgG1 Fc (primary comparator): The study used an intact monoclonal 8.8 triple mutant [mAb 8.8 TM] that contains the same triple mutated IgG1 Fc portion but has no known binding specificity. Rituxan served as positive control.
Marstacimab does not bind to C1q, suggesting that it is unlikely to activate the classical complement pathway.
Marstacimab binding to all FcγRs, was similar to, or lower than isotype control mAb 8.8 TM and lower than positive control. Marstacimab binding to all FcγRs, was lower than the positive control (IgG1- keyhole limpet hemocyanin).
Conclusion: Marstacimab has low potential to elicit antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity activity. Marstacimab was negative for C1q binding, suggesting it is unlikely to activate the classical complement pathway and cause complement dependent cytotoxicity.

L859: ## 13.1.2.1. Safety Pharmacology
L860: Safety pharmacology assessments were incorporated into the 3-month toxicology study in monkeys (Table 49). Intravenous administration of marstacimab at doses ranging from 30 to 500 mg/kg/week and SC administration of 90 mg/kg/week to monkeys had no effect on respiratory, neurological, or cardiovascular parameters. There was no change in QTc interval following intravenous administration of 500 mg/kg/week marstacimab in monkeys.

L865: ## 13.1.2.1.1. ADME/Pharmacokinetics
L866: The pharmacokinetics (PK) and toxicokinetics (TK) of marstacimab were characterized following once weekly subcutaneous and intravenous administrations in both Wistar Han rats and cynomolgus monkeys. Single and repeat dose PK (Study #13MA070, 8299672) and repeat dose TK studies (Studies #20064198, #20062114, #20089324) showed marstacimab exposure increased with increases in dose in both rats and monkeys. Importantly, marstacimab is cleared by target-mediated drug disposition. Low doses are rapidly cleared by target, while higher doses saturate target-mediated clearance, leading increasing half-life (t_{1/2}). The t_{1/2} for marstacimab at 1 mg/kg intravenous (IV) was 12.3 hours in cynomolgus monkeys but was extended to 118 hours at 10 mg/kg IV (Table 50 and Table 51). The volume of distribution (Vd) in the monkey was estimated to be 34 to 65 mL/kg, which is similar the Vd of other monoclonal antibodies.
L867: **Table 50. Pharmacokinetic Parameters on Day 1 After Single IV Dose of Marstacimab in Rats and Monkeys**

L874: “—” indicates unable to determine
L875: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; CL, clearance; IV, intravenous;
L876: PK/PD, pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic; T1/2, elimination half-life; TK, toxicokinetic; Vss, volume of distribution at steady-state
L877: Administration of marstacimab in combination with other compounds had no impact on marstacimab levels in rats suggesting drug-drug interaction is highly unlikely. No specific metabolism, protein binding, or excretion studies were conducted in nonclinical species. Marstacimab is not expected to bind to albumin or \alpha-1 acid glycoprotein. Marstacimab is predicted to be metabolized by a proteomic catabolism. Clearance of marstacimab follows a target-mediated drug disposition (TMDD), which predicts that higher affinity will result in higher clearance. Since marstacimab has lower affinity to TFPI than concizumab, larger doses should be necessary to produce a similar pharmacodynamic (PD) effect as concizumab. TMDD theory also predicts that lower doses will be cleared more rapidly while larger saturating doses will lead to a longer half-life. The low potency of marstacimab relative to concizumab (7-fold lower) thus requires a larger dose to achieve therapeutic efficacy, which results in a longer half-life that allows less frequent dosing of marstacimab in both nonclinical species (once weekly) and humans (once weekly mg) compared to concizumab.

L884: ## **13.1.2.2. Toxicokinetic Parameters**
L885: TK parameters with administration of marstacimab by the SC and IV routes to Wistar Han rats and Cynomolgus monkey are shown in Table 52, Table 53, and Table 54.
L886: Plasma levels of marstacimab in nonclinical species were measured using a validated biotin-labeled mouse anti-marstacimab monoclonal antibody on streptavidin coated microtiter plate and detected using a ruthenium-labeled mouse anti-human IgG Fc antibody. Analysis of plasma samples for antidrug antibody (ADA) detected ADA in a minority of rats (~28%) in the 3-month study found no evidence of marstacimab neutralization (Table 52). There was no evidence of ADA in the 3-month monkey or 6-month rat studies. Marstacimab's systemic exposure increased with dose in a slightly greater than dose-proportional manner on Days 29 and 85 in the 3-month rat study but in a dose-proportional manner in the 3-month monkey and 6-month rat studies. There were no sex-related differences in drug exposures in rats or monkeys. Following repeated SC dosing of marstacimab, time to maximum plasma concentration (T_{max}) ranged between 48 to 63 hours in rats and from 41 to 64 hours in monkeys.

L903: ## 13.1.2.2.1. General Toxicology
L904: Marstacimab produced a clear pharmacodynamic response in mouse hemophilia models. While marstacimab increased D-dimer, a marker of blood clot formation, in healthy rats and monkeys, administration of marstacimab did not decrease prothrombin time, APTT, or increase fibrin. No histopathological evidence of bleeding was observed at 200-times the area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) in the 3-month rat study (doses up to 1000 mg/kg/week) or 3-month monkey study (doses up to 500 mg/kg/week). However, minimal acute emboli were detected in the 6-month rat study in the lung at 60 (1F, 1M), 180 (4F, 2M) and 1000 mg/kg/week (1F, 0M) doses and in tail injection sites at 180 (2F, 1M) and 1000 mg/kg/week (2F, 1M). Neither the lung nor the tail injection site thrombi/emboli findings in the 6-month rat study were considered adverse based on (a) absence of secondary lung tissue injury, (b) lack of clear dose-response, and (c) absence of change in prothrombin time, a marker of extrinsic and common pathway integrity. Similar acute minimal incidences of lung emboli were observed at the low dose of 30 mg/kg/week in one monkey (3-month study) and in one male rat (pharmacology study) at 50 mg/kg. In none of the cases was there any evidence of secondary lung injury, suggesting the lung emboli likely occurred at a very low level or at the study termination, possibly due to combined effect of marstacimab plus stress (exsanguination). Despite the unclear relationship between lung emboli in healthy animals and marstacimab, the anticipated risk of thrombi/emboli in hemophilia patients cannot be dismissed.
L905: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L906: In summary, the presence of marstacimab-related acute minimal lung and tail thrombi/emboli identified sporadically across studies, suggests that marstacimab contributes to a minimally pro-coagulatory state in healthy animals that remains in homeostasis under most conditions. Thus, the top weekly intravenous dose of 1000 mg/kg/dose in rats and 500 mg/kg/dose in monkeys were regarded as the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) doses, with AUC exposures approximately 200-times greater than clinical exposure at the maximum recommended human dose (Table 55).

L915: ## **Study #20064198 / Title: A 3-Month Intravenous or Subcutaneous Injection Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study of PF-06741086 in Rats With a 6-Week Recovery Phase**
L916: ## **Key Study Findings**
L917: - Minimal, nondose dependent decreases in prothrombin time, with an unexpected prolongation of APTT, and lower fibrin concentrations, were observed during the study but not at the end of the recovery.
- Marstacimab was not associated any adverse histopathology findings in rats at SC doses up to 180 mg/kg/week or IV doses up to 1000 mg/kg/wk.
- ADAs were detected in 9/32 (28.1%) treated animals but had no impact on marstacimab exposure.
- With no marstacimab related adverse findings, the highest 1000 mg/kg/week IV and 180 mg/kg/week SC doses were accepted as the NOAELs in the 3-month rat study. Exposure at the SC NOAEL dose were 5-times the exposure at the MRHD on an AUC basis.
L918: ## **Study #20062114 / Title: A 3-Month Intravenous or Subcutaneous Injection Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study of PF-06741086 in Cynomolgus Monkeys With a 6-Week Recovery Phase**
L919: ## **Key Study Findings**
L920: - Contrary to expected pharmacodynamic effect of marstacimab, there was an increase in prothrombin time and aPTT and decrease in fibrinogen concentration (FIB) concentrations (~60%). There was, however, a significant increase in D-dimer concentration (DDIM) (marker of blood clot) that supported pharmacological activity.
- There was a single minimal acute incidence of lung emboli at low dose of 30 mg/kg/week IV.
- There were no detectable ADAs in marstacimab treated monkeys given by IV or SC route.
- The highest doses of 500 mg/kg/wk IV and 90 mg/kg/week SC were considered the NOAEL doses. Exposures at the IV NOAEL were approximately 219X the exposure at the MRHD dose on an AUC basis. Exposures at the highest SC dose of 90 mg/kg were roughly 40X the exposure at the MRHD on an AUC basis.
L921: ## **Study #20089324 / Title: A 6-Month Intravenous Bolus Injection Toxicity Study of PF-06741086 in Rats With a 6-Week Recovery Period**
L922: ## **Key Study Findings**
L923: - Contrary to expected pharmacodynamic effect, marstacimab increased prothrombin time at HD of 1000 mg/kg/week in both sexes and increased APTT (all doses) and decreased FIB (all doses). DDIM levels, a marker of increase in risk of blood-clot formation was increased at all doses.
- Minimal acute thrombi/emboli with no secondary tissue injury was observed in the lungs and tail vein injection sites at \ge 60 mg/kg/week.
- In contrast to the results of the 3-month rat study, there were no detectable ADAs in the 6-month rat study. Based on the minimal emboli being considered non-adverse, the highest IV dose of 1000 mg/kg/week was considered the NOAEL in rats (approximately 200X the exposure at the MRHD on an AUC basis).
L924: ## **13.1.2.2.2. Genetic Toxicology**
L925: As a monoclonal antibody, genotoxicity assessment of marstacimab was not scientifically appropriate and therefore was not conducted.
L926: ## 13.1.2.2.3. Carcinogenicity
L927: As a biological compound, the genotoxicity of marstacimab was evaluated based on a weight of evidence (WoE) approach, the key points of which are highlighted below:
L928: - Biological nature of marstacimab: Biologicals possess no inherent carcinogenic risk based on chemical structure or metabolism.
- Mechanism of action/therapeutic target: Marstacimab is an inhibitor of TFPI, a blood clotting regulator with no identified role in carcinogenic pathways.
- Toxicology data: No preneoplastic lesions, evidence of hormonal perturbation, or signs of immune modulation were observed across studies in animals.
L929: Based on the WoE provided by the Applicant, the Division agreed that marstacimab is unlikely to pose a significant carcinogenicity safety concern for humans and therefore considered a 2-year study in rats unlikely to provide additional useful information.
L930: ## 13.1.2.2.4. Reproductive Toxicology
L931: ## **Study #00655204 / Title: A Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Study of PF-06741086 by Intravenous Injection in Male Wistar Rats**
L932: ## Key Study Findings
L933: - Males Wistar rats dosed intravenously once weekly with marstacimab up to 1000 mg/kg (total of 11 doses) showed no adverse effects on fertility.
- Marstacimab treatment in males mated with treatment-naïve females did not affect pregnancy, implantation, or embryonic survival.
- The highest once weekly dose of 1000 mg/kg marstacimab was accepted as the NOAEL. Although drug exposures were not determined, plasma concentrations of marstacimab measured at 5 minutes postdose supported a determination of adequate drug exposure, because values were consistent with existing TK data. Based on existing AUC data, exposure at the NOAEL in the study was likely 201-times the exposure at the MRHD of 300 mg per week on an AUC basis.

L939: ## Impurities/Degradants
L940: As a biological compound, marstacimab impurities consisted of process-related impurities, product-related impurities, and potential contaminant in the drug substance. Process-related impurities originated from manufacturing process which consisted of host cell proteins, host cell DNA and cell culture media components and impurities related to downstream processing of marstacimab. The residual DNA impurities were DNA fragments (CHO cell line), cell culture media (chromatography purification) and contaminants (endotoxin, bioburden, mycoplasma, viruses). These impurities were at very low levels, not greater than the quantification limit for all except for host cell protein. Table 57 lists the process-related impurities evaluated for marstacimab.

L946: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L947: - Marstacimab batches used in toxicology studies contained similar or lower levels of impurities compared to the clinical batches. Safety of process-related impurities was therefore addressed in toxicology studies.
- The process-related residual DNA content/impurities detected in (b) (4) (commercial manufacturing process) of process performance qualification (PPQ) were less (b) (4) pg/mg.
- The host cell protein content in process (b) (4) batches were no more than (b) (4) ng/mg, the limit of quantification of LC/MS/MS.
- (b) (4) leaching from the chromatography column in the process (b) (4) was no more than (b) (4) ng/mg, the quantitation limit.
L948: Overall, impurities were qualified and within the marstacimab drug substance specifications. Since the commercial manufacturing process (b) (4) batches had lower impurities than those tested in nonclinical studies, the safety of the impurities is adequately addressed, and no safety concern was identified.
L949: ## Excipients in Drug Product
L950: Marstacimab is an IgG1 lambda human mAb with two identical heavy chains and two identical light chains, covalently linked with four inter-chain disulfide bonds. To be marketed marstacimab is a sterile, preservative free and colorless drug product (150 mg/mL, pH 5.8) formulated for subcutaneous injection in a 1 mL prefilled single dose disposable glass (Type I (b) (4) glass) syringe pen with a 27-gauge thin walled 1/2-inch needle with a chlorobutyl elastomer plunger stopper.
L951: Excipients used in marstacimab clinical formulation are all generally regarded as safe (L-histidine and L-histidine monohydrochloride, sucrose, disodium edetate, polysorbate 80 (Table 58)). Marstacimab is sensitive to light therefore susceptible to photodegradation. All excipients had been used at similar or higher concentrations with other injectables (Table 59).

L981: ## Leachability Study
L982: The leachability study designed to identify the daily exposure to leachable compounds from the container (glass syringe and chlorobutyl plunger stopper) was based on notable compounds detected in the extractability study. The safety concern threshold (SCT) defined as 1.5 µg/day TDI for each mutagenic and 5 µg/day for non-mutagenic compounds per International Council for Harmonisation M7(R1) was applied to safety assessment of leachable compounds. Any compound exceeding 1.5 µg/day was identified and quantitated. Leachability studies have been completed for up to 12 months and long-term studies up to 60 months are ongoing. All organic compounds in the 6-month leachability tests were below the quantification limit with the exception of [REDACTED] (b) (4) at [REDACTED] (b) (4) µg/day (Table 62). Since all TDIs were lower than PDE (International Council for Harmonisation Q3C) and have established toxicological data by all routes, the Applicant did not carry any further risk assessment. However, the chemistry, manufacturing, and controls reviewer considered [REDACTED] (b) (4) as potential concern and requested further safety assessment.
L983: **Table 62. Leachable Results for Drug Product Lot EH7901 PFS Stored at 2 to 8 °C: Leachable Studies and Data for 24, 36, 48 and 60 Months Yet To Be Determined**
L984: (b) (4)
L985: ## Conclusion
L986: In summary, compounds identified in the leachability studies coming from glass syringe and chlorobutyl plunger stopper were within the acceptable daily limits and do not pose a safety risk to humans. It should be noted that both the glass barrel pen and chlorobutyl plunger stopper are obtained from commercial sources and have been used with similar products.
L987: ## 13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted With the New Drug Application
L988: ## Study #200064198 /Title: A 3-Month Intravenous or Subcutaneous Injection Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study of PF-06741086 in Rats With a 6-Week Recovery Phase
L989: ## Key Study Findings
L990: - Minimal and nondose dependent decrease in prothrombin time, prolongation of APTT (at all doses), and lower FIB concentrations were observed during the study but not at the end of the recovery.
- There were no marstacimab associated adverse histopathology findings in rats.
- Non-neutralizing ADAs were detected in 9/32 (28.1%) treated animals (no change in drug exposure).
- No marstacimab related adverse findings were seen in rats. The top doses of 1000 mg/kg once weekly IV and 180 mg/kg once weekly SC were considered NOAELs in rats. Exposure at the NOAEL dose was 212X the exposure at the MRHD on AUC basis.

- There were no adverse histopathology findings in monkeys. The top doses of 500 mg/kg/wk IV and 90 mg/kg/wk SC were considered NOAELs. Exposure at the IV NOAEL was about

L1040: ## **Study #20089324 /Title: A 6-Month Intravenous Bolus Injection Toxicity Study of PF-06741086 in Rats With a 6-Week Recovery Period**
L1041: ## **Key Study Findings**
L1042: - Marstacimab increased prothrombin time at 1000 mg/kg/wk in both sexes and contrary to pro-coagulatory effect, marstacimab increased APTT and DDIM and decreased FIB at all doses in both sexes
- Minimal Acute marstacimab-related evidence of thrombi/emboli with no secondary tissue injury was observed the lungs and tail vein injection sites at \ge 60 mg/kg/wk.
- In contrast to the 3-month rat study, there were no detectable ADA in the 6-month rat study. Based on minimal nonadverse findings, the top weekly IV dose of 1000 mg/kg (AUC_{168h}=1,840,000 \mug.h/mL) was considered the NOAELs in rats and the exposure was 201X the exposure at the MRHD on AUC basis (AUC_{168h} 9146 \mug.h/mL).

Adequate battery: Yes	The lung findings included acute minimal emboli in males (C:0, LD:2, MD3, HD:1) and females (C:0, LD:1, MD:0, HD:4), minimal cellular infiltrate with refractile material in the lung, and minimal basophilic or nonbasophilic (light gray or clear) foreign material in the lung at \ge 60 mg/kg/dose in males and/or females. There were no such findings in the 3m rat or monkeys treated with marstacimab doses up to 1000 mg/kg/wk.
In the tail vain, the minimal thrombi/emboli (acute and organizing) were observed at the injection site at \ge 180 mg/kg/dose in males and at \ge 60 mg/kg/dose in females.
Acute minimal thrombi/emboli at the injection site were characterized by pale staining eosinophilic granular to fibrillar material within the very small vessel. Although thrombi/emboli are common at venipuncture sites; the minimal increase in incidence in the emboli in lungs compared with controls appear to correspond with the expected drug pharmacology, however, in absence of secondary effect at the site of emboli and increase in PT and aPTT, the relation to marstacimab is not clear. Based on minimal incidence and acute nature of emboli in some treated animals, it is highly possible that emboli were triggered during the last stage of the study, i.e., euthanasia/exsanguination.

L1067: ## 13.2.1. Reproductive Toxicology
L1068: ## **Study #00655204 / Title: A Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Study of PF-06741086 by Intravenous Injection in Male Wistar Rats**
L1069: ## Key Study Findings
L1070: - Marstacimab treated males up to 1000 mg/kg/wk, mated with native females did not affect male reproductive performance or spermatogenesis neither pregnancy, implantation, nor embryonic survival in untreated pregnant females.
- Plasma concentration at 5 min post 1000 mg/kg in male rats (5,080,000 ng/mL) was consistent with drug exposure in toxicity studies, thus AUC for exposure multiple was based on 6-month rat study. Based on existing AUC data, the exposure in males was estimated to be 212X the exposure at the MRHD of 300 mg on AUC basis.
- The top dose of 1000 mg/kg/wk was accepted as NOAEL. Exposure at the NOAEL dose was 201X the exposure at the MRHD of 300 mg per week on AUC basis.
L1071: Good Laboratory Practice compliance: Yes
L1072: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection

Study design	Males Wistar rats dosed intravenously once weekly with marstacimab up to 1000 mg/kg (total 11 doses, at least 4 wks prior to mating). Plasma samples were collected at 5 min post dose. Blood samples collected at the end of the study from males was used to determine marstacimab exposure.
Deviation from study protocol affecting the interpretation of results	Minor with no impact on the study. Plasma samples collected for ADA analysis were not evaluated. Previous studies have shown no notable ADA in rats treated with marstacimab for up to 6 months.

L1077: **Table 77. Observations and Results, Study #00655204**
L1078: Parameter	Major Findings
Mortality	No deaths or effect on survival
Clinical signs	No marstacimab-associated clinical signs
Body weights	No adverse or significant changes
Reproductive performance	Marstacimab had no effect in male rats' reproductive performance, i.e., mating, fertility and copulation indices or pre-coital intervals. Embryonic data collected from untreated females mated with marstacimab-treated males found no marstacimab-related changes in number of viable embryos, total resorptions, pre- or post-implantation loss, corpora lutea or number of gravid females at necropsy at the study termination.
Spermatogenic evaluation	Marstacimab had no effect on spermatogenesis endpoints, i.e., mean cauda epididymal sperm numbers or sperm motility, epididymal weight
Necropsy findings	There were no gross or microscopic findings in male rats.
Offspring	Marstacimab treated males mated with native females did not affect the pregnancy, implantation, or embryonic survival in untreated pregnant females. The top weekly IV dose of 1000 mg/kg was considered NOAEL.
PK analysis	Plasma concentrations of marstacimab were determined at 5 min post dose on Day 70 (plasma 50,800,000 ng/mL). No additional samples were collected or analyzed for ADA.
Based on existing 3-month rat PK data at 1000 mg/kg., the exposure multiples provided by 1000 mg/kg marstacimab was greater than 200-times the exposure at the MRHD of 300 mg on AUC basis.

L1081: ## 14. Clinical Pharmacology
L1082: ## 14.1. In Vitro Studies
L1083: In patients with hemophilia who develop inhibitors4, the Applicant proposes that bypass agents5, such as recombinant Factor VIIa (rFVIIa) or activated prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC) may be used to treat breakthrough bleeds while on marstacimab prophylaxis. Therefore, the Applicant conducted in vitro drug-drug interaction studies to evaluate the potential additive effect of marstacimab and the bypass agents on coagulation parameters.
L1084: ## Marstacimab When Used in Combination With rFVIIa, Study #122443
L1085: The additive effect of marstacimab and rFVIIa on thrombin generation in hemophilic plasma with or without inhibitors was evaluated using the TGA. The peak thrombin levels when rFVIIa and marstacimab were administered alone or in combination in plasma of patients with HA with or without inhibitor, and HB without inhibitor are provided in Table 78. Marstacimab concentration of 16 µg/mL was selected to approximate steady state maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}) based on modeling of 2 mg/kg subcutaneous dose. The C_{max,ss} based on the population PK modeling of 150 mg SC weekly dose (with loading dose of 300 mg) is 19.7 µg/mL. The peak thrombin level when marstacimab was administered alone (82 to 172nM, 88 to 166nM, and 174nM) or in combination with rFVIIa (102 to 178nM, 99 to 57nM, and 167nM) was comparable in plasma of patients HA with or without inhibitors and HB without inhibitor, respectively, demonstrating minimal additive effect.

L1093: ## **Marstacimab When Used in Combination With aPCC, Study #045123**
L1094: The effect of marstacimab in promoting coagulation when used in combination with plasma derived aPCC was evaluated using the TGA in platelet poor plasma from patients with HA with inhibitor and patients with HB with inhibitor. The peak thrombin level using the TGA is shown in Table 79. The peak thrombin levels when marstacimab was used in combination with aPCC (209.3 to 240.6nM and 254.1nM in hemophilia A and B, respectively) were higher than marstacimab (72.9 to 109.7nM and 70.1nM in hemophilia A and B, respectively) or aPCC (74.7 to 110.1nM and 163.1nM in hemophilia A and B, respectively) alone. However, the thrombin levels when used in combination were still within the range of marstacimab in nonhemophilic plasma (182.1 to 256.7nM and 160.4nM in hemophilia A and B, respectively) which would have full complement of coagulation factors.

L1100: ## 14.2. In Vivo Studies
L1101: ## Study B7841001
L1102: A randomized, double-blind, sponsor-open, placebo-controlled, single IV or SC dose escalation study to evaluate the safety, tolerability, PK, and PD of PF-06741086 (marstacimab) in healthy participants and an open-label evaluation in healthy Japanese participants.
L1103: ## Primary Objective
L1104: To determine the safety and tolerability of marstacimab administered to healthy participants as single escalating SC administered doses or single escalating IV infused doses.
L1105: ## Study Design
L1106: This was a first-in-human, randomized, double-blind, sponsor-open, placebo-controlled study to investigate the safety, tolerability, PK, and PD of SC and IV administration of marstacimab. The study was conducted in seven planned cohorts of healthy male adult participants (Cohorts 1 through 7) and in an additional cohort of healthy Japanese male participants (Cohort 8). Participants were enrolled in a dose escalating fashion and randomized to either marstacimab or placebo (3:1 ratio) at doses of 30 mg (N=4), 100 mg (N=8), and 300 mg (N=8) subcutaneously
L1107: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L1108: and 150 mg (N=8), 300 mg (N=8), and 440 mg (N=8) intravenously. Japanese participants were enrolled into an additional single dose cohort (N=4) at marstacimab 300 mg SC. The pharmacokinetic sampling were done at predose, and 1, 4, 12, 24, 48, 72 hours, and 7, 14, 21, 28, 35, 42, 56, 70, and 84 days after SC dosing and at predose, and 1, 2, 4, 8, 12, 24, 48, 72 hours, and 7, 14, 21, 28, 35, 42, 56, 70, and 84 days after IV dosing. The pharmacodynamic samples such as TFPI level, TGA, prothrombin fragment 1+2, D-dimer, and diluted prothrombin time were collected at the same time as PK samples. The immunogenicity samples were collected at predose and 14, 21, 28, 35, 42, 56, 70, and 84 days postdose.
L1109: ## Results
L1110: Summary of PK parameters for marstacimab following single dose administration are provided in Table 80. The median plasma marstacimab concentration versus time profiles for SC and IV administration are provided in Figure 19. The PK parameters were not determined for the 30 mg SC cohort due to all concentrations below limit of quantification (BLQ). The Applicant noted that participants in the 150 mg IV cohort were slightly under-dosed due to an incorrect pump setting; however, the actual dose of 147 mg was accurately captured. The median time to reach Cmax was 48 to 108 hours following SC administration. Regardless of route of administration (SC or IV), the exposure of marstacimab (Cmax and AUC) increased with increasing dose in a greater than dose proportional manner. The clearance also decreased with increasing dose and the half-life increased from 33.3 hours to 65.8 hrs following 100 mg and 300 mg SC administration, respectively. The nonlinear PK is likely due to the target-mediated drug disposition. The exposure was comparable between Japanese and non-Japanese participants following 300 mg SC administration.

L1153: ## Study B7841002
L1154: A multicenter, open-label, multiple ascending dose study to evaluate the safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, and efficacy of subcutaneous or intravenous PF-06741086 in participants with severe hemophilia.
L1155: ## Primary Objective
L1156: To determine the safety and tolerability of multiple doses of PF-06741086 administered to severe hemophilia A and B participants with and without inhibitors.
L1157: ## Study Design
L1158: This was a Phase 1b/2, open-label, multiple ascending dose study in male participants with severe hemophilia A or B, with or without inhibitors. The study consisted of four cohorts, starting at marstacimab 300 mg SC once weekly (Cohort 1, N=8, noninhibitors). Based on the safety, tolerability, and PK data through Day 29, lower dose of 150 mg SC once weekly with a loading dose of 300 mg (Cohort 2, N=6, noninhibitors) and higher dose of 450 mg SC once weekly (Cohort 3, N=6, noninhibitors) were initiated. Cohort 4 was originally intended for studying marstacimab 1000 mg IV once a month dosing regimen, however, Cohort 4 was modified to receive marstacimab 300 mg SC once weekly for participants with inhibitors only (N=7). The participants received treatment up to 3 months. The pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic sampling was done on Days 1, 2, 4, 8, 15, 22, 29, 30, 33, 57, 85, and 113. The immunogenicity sampling was done on Days 1, 15, 29, 57, 85 and 113.
L1159: ## Results
L1160: Summary of PK parameters following weekly marstacimab doses are provided in **Table 83**. The median plasma marstacimab concentration versus time profiles for marstacimab are provided in **Figure 25**. After multiple doses on Day 29 (Week 5), exposure (AUC_{\tau} and C_{max}) increased greater than dose-proportional over the range of 150 mg to 450 mg. Based on the trough concentrations, steady state appeared to have been achieved by Day 57. There was no significant difference in the exposure between participants with or without inhibitors based on marstacimab 300 mg weekly dose.

L1172: Treatment related changes in PD parameters were also evaluated. The median absolute values (TFPI, peak TGA and D-dimer) or median changes from baseline (TGA lag time, diluted prothrombin time and prothrombin fragment 1+2) versus time are provided in Figure 26. The
L1173: ## HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
L1174: total TFPI increased with increase in dose, consistent with the binding of free TFPI with marstacimab, resulting in delayed elimination of bound TFPI. For other PD parameters, although response to treatment was observed (e.g., increase in TGA peak, prothrombin fragments 1+2, D-dimer and decrease in TGA lag time, diluted prothrombin time) there was no consistent dose-related effect observed and variability was high.

L1179: We concluded as there were no consistent dose-dependent changes observed in PD parameters, especially peak thrombin generation, PD changes were not considered when evaluating the dose selection. Evaluation of Study B7841002 for Phase 3 dose selection is provided in Section 6.1.
L1180: A total of three participants had treatment-induced ADA (11.5%, 3/26). Among the three participants, no participant tested positive for neutralizing antibody (NAb).
L1181: ## Study B7841009
L1182: A Phase 1, open-label, randomized, 4-period, 2-sequence, crossover study to evaluate the bioequivalence of marstacimab (PF-06741086) PFS device and PFP device following subcutaneous administration in healthy adult male participants.
L1183: ## Primary Objective
L1184: To compare the single dose PK of marstacimab (300 mg) administered subcutaneously with a PFP device to that with the PFS device in healthy adult male participants.
L1185: ## Study Design
L1186: This was a Phase 1, open-label, randomized, single dose, 4-period, 2-sequence, fully replicate crossover study in healthy adult male participants. A single dose of 300 mg marstacimab (given as two single injections of 150 mg) was administered subcutaneously into the abdomen on Day 1 of each period, following a standardized breakfast. Dose administration in each of the four period was alternated between the top half and bottom half of the abdomen. The pharmacokinetic sampling was conducted at predose, and 1, 2, 4, 8, 12, 24, 48, 72 hours, and 7, 14, 21 days after dosing. The immunogenicity samples were collected at predose (on Day 1 prior to dosing) and on Day 21.
L1187: ## Results
L1188: The study was terminated early due to a serious adverse event (SAE) of pulmonary embolism in one participant. A total of 18 participants completed at least 1 period and contributed to data for calculation of PK parameters. Summary of mean plasma PK parameters and statistical comparison of PFS and PFP are provided in Table 84 and Table 85. The plasma concentration versus time curve is shown in Figure 27. The ratios (90% CI) of geometric means of C_{max} and AUC_{last} were 104.1% (93.7 to 115.6%) and 107.5% (95.2 to 121.4%) showing comparable exposure when administered via PFP and PFS. The median time to reach maximum concentration was 72 hours for both PFP and PFS with overlapping range of 60 to 146 hours. As the sample size was smaller than planned, a sensitivity analysis was conducted using data from participants who completed at least 2 periods (N=15). The results showed that the geometric mean ratios of C_{max} and AUC_{last} were still comparable (C_{max}: 107.7% [98.8 to 117.5%] and AUC: 111.9% [101.1 to 123.9%]). A total of six participants tested positive for ADAs and of those, two participants positive for NAb.

L1202: ## Study B7841010
L1203: A Phase 1, single-arm, open-label, nonrandomized, noncontrolled multicenter study to evaluate the pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, safety, and tolerability of a single subcutaneous dose of PF-06741086 in Chinese adult participants with severe hemophilia.
L1204: ## **Primary Objective**
L1205: To determine the safety and tolerability of a single SC dose of marstacimab administered to Chinese adult participants with severe hemophilia A or B with or without inhibitors.
L1206: ## **Study Design**
L1207: This study was a single-arm, open-label, nonrandomized, noncontrolled, multicenter study in Chinese adult participants between ages 18 to &lt;75 years with severe hemophilia A or B, with or without inhibitor. All participants (N=6) received a single subcutaneous injection of marstacimab 300 mg (2 x 150 mg) on Day 1. The pharmacokinetic and PD blood samples were collected at predose and 1, 4, 12, 24, 48, 72 hours and 14, 21, 28 days postdose. The immunogenicity samples were collected at predose and Days 14, 21, and 28 of the study.
L1208: ## Results
L1209: Summary of plasma marstacimab PK parameters are provided in Table 86. Median plasma marstacimab concentration-time profile is shown in Figure 28. Treatment-related changes were observed in all PD markers (i.e., increase in total TFPI, prothrombin fragment 1+2, d-dimer, TGA peak and decrease in dPT and TGA lag time). Two participants had tested positive for ADA but negative for NAb.

L1218: ## 14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and Performance
L1219: ## 14.3.1. Bioanalytical Method for PK Assessment
L1220: Plasma marstacimab concentrations were assayed using a validated electrochemiluminescence (ECL) method. Standards, controls, and test samples were incubated with TFPI (1.0 µg/mL), which has been immobilized on a 96-well MSD standard plate. After incubation, unbound material is washed away and marstacimab bound to the plate is incubated with a primary detection antibody, Mu anti-TFPI Ab. Following incubation, the plate is washed and bound marstacimab is incubated with a secondary detection antibody, goat anti-mouse IgG Sulfo TAG (1.0 µg/mL). After a final wash, 2X read buffer containing tripropylamine is added immediately prior to reading the plate on an MSD Sector Imager 600 plate reader generating a chemiluminescent signal. The signal is directly proportional to the amount of marstacimab in the sample.
L1221: An overview of marstacimab ECL method validation parameters and assay performance are provided in Table 87 and Table 88. For all clinical studies, 2 ECL methods were developed and validated (i.e., method (b) (4) 15-1536 for Studies B7841001, B7841002, B7841003, B7841005, B7841007 and B7841009 and method (b) (4) P7615MVHuP1AP for Study B7841005 and
L1222: B7841010). The Applicant provided cross validation results that demonstrated equivalency of the two methods. The method validation and sample analysis are deemed adequate.

L1236: ## 14.3.2. Bioanalytical Method for PD Assessment
L1237: For PD characterization, plasma samples were analyzed for total TFPI, TGA (comprised of lag time, peak thrombin and endogenous thrombin generation potential), d-dimer, prothrombin fragment (PF) 1+2, dPT. All PD parameters were measured using laboratory developed test validated under CAP/CLIA requirements except for total TFPI, which was analyzed using validated bioanalytical method. Total TFPI, which includes marstacimab bound and unbound forms, was extracted from human plasma using a biotinylated noncompeting anti-TFPI antibody. This TFPI antibody complex was captured using streptavidin magnetic beads. Because TFPI is an endogenous component in human plasma, the method for TFPI quantitation was validated in mouse plasma as surrogate matrix.
L1238: ## 14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety
L1239: ## 14.4.1. Summary of Clinical Studies
L1240: Immunogenicity of marstacimab was evaluated in three Phase 1 studies (Studies B7841009, B7841010, B7841001), two Phase 1/2 studies (Studies B7841002 and B7841003), and three Phase 3 studies (Studies B7841005, B7841007, B7841008). Among the Phase 3 studies, only Study B7841005 has available immunogenicity information, which is specifically from one of two subgroups, the noninhibitor subgroup because the study of inhibitor subgroup is still ongoing. Therefore, information in the draft label is coming from the noninhibitor cohort of the Phase 3 Study B7841005. The submitted clinical study results included participants who received 12-month treatment. **Table 89** contains the assessment of immunogenicity information up to 12 months for Study B7841005. Overall study design in terms of treatment duration and sampling time and size was adequate to assess the ADA impact on PK.

L1245: ## 14.4.2. Highlight of Key Characteristics of Immunogenicity Assays Relevant to This Review
L1246: Table 90 and Table 91 show several assay characteristics of the ADA and NAb assay respectively, that are relevant for the analysis.
L1247: The ADA assay can detect 100 ng/mL of positive control in the presence of 25 µg/mL of marstacimab. A fraction (16%) of the ADA samples had drug concentration greater than the ADA drug tolerance (25 µg/mL). Therefore, the presence of marstacimab may interfere with detection of ADA in 16% of study samples.
L1248: The NAb assay can detect 1200 ng/mL of positive control in the presence of 50 µg/mL of marstacimab. A fraction (2.6%) of the NAb samples had drug concentration greater than the NAb drug tolerance of 50 mg/mL. Therefore, the presence of marstacimab may interfere with detection of NAb in 2.6% of study samples.

L1257: ## 14.4.3. Methods for Evaluating the Effect of Immunogenicity on PK of Marstacimab
L1258: To evaluate the impact of immunogenicity on PK, the observed concentrations in two groups (ADA+ and ADA-) was compared with time-matched PK and ADA data using Immunogenicity Specimen (IS) tool developed in-house. At each timepoint, marstacimab geometric mean concentrations were tabulated by ADA+ and ADA- groups. For the statistical comparisons of concentration data between ADA+ and ADA- groups, the geometric mean ratio (GMR) of ADA+/ADA- and the corresponding 90% CI are presented.
L1259: This review does not include the evaluation of NAb effect on PK.
L1260: ## 14.4.4. Effect of Immunogenicity on PK of Marstacimab – Results
L1261: The results of the IS analysis in Study B7841005, specifically, the effect of ADA on PK is presented. Table 92 summarizes the marstacimab geometric mean concentrations in ADA+ and ADA- participants during the treatment period. The analysis of marstacimab concentration from ADA+ and ADA- samples during the treatment period and 90% confidence interval of GMR of drug concentration at each visit is provided in Figure 29. Marstacimab concentration is lower in ADA+ participants. The IS analysis results show that the GMR (ADA+/ADA-) ranged from 0.50 to 0.76 through the study duration of 360 days. In addition, the 90% CI of the GMR is entirely below 1.0 at 6 of 8 time points over the study duration (i.e., except for Days 180 and 300). Therefore, development of ADA decreases the concentration of marstacimab.

L1270: ## 14.4.5. Effect of Immunogenicity on Efficacy of Marstacimab
L1271: The impact of immunogenicity on efficacy of marstacimab was evaluated based on comparison of the clinical outcome (i.e., annualized bleeding rate [ABR]) in ADA+ and ADA- participants. The summary of the ABR of treated bleeds (primary endpoint) by ADA status is provided in Table 93. Based on the results, the ADA impact on marstacimab PK did not affect clinical outcome. The mean ABR was 3.34 for ADA+ participants whereas the mean ABR was 4.92 in ADA- participants.

L1276: ## 14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
L1277: ## 14.5.1. Summary of Applicant's Population PK and Pharmacodynamic Modeling Analyses
L1278: Both population pharmacokinetic (PPK) and pharmacodynamic (PKPD) modeling analyses were conducted to support the dose selection for adult and adolescent participants with hemophilia. The objectives of Applicant's modeling analysis were:
L1279: - To develop an integrated, predictive PPK model for marstacimab plasma drug and TFPI concentrations.
- To identify potential covariates which impact marstacimab concentration levels.
- To explore the relationship between free target (TFPI) and peak thrombin.
L1280: ## Method
L1281: NONMEM version 7.5.0 was used for all model estimations. Perl-speaks-NONMEM (PsN) version 5.3.0 was used for visual predictive check (VPC) and/or bootstrap simulations; R 4.1.3 was used for pre- and post-processing and for summarizing and plotting results. R package mrgsolve (version 1.0.4) was used for generating simulated PK and PKPD results. Marstacimab PK was described using a TMDD model defined in terms of clearance (CL), V_c, Q, V_p, first-order absorption rate constant (k_a), and absorption lag time (T_{lag}). Both drug concentration data and total receptor (TFPI) data were used to estimate model parameters. Separate residual error models were included in the model for PK and total TFPI. A PKPD model was developed to describe peak thrombin and its relationship to free TFPI predicted from the final PPK model using an E_{max} type of model. At all stages of model development, goodness-of-fit plots were examined to assess model adequacy, possible lack of fit, or violation of assumptions.
L1282: ## Results
L1283: The PPK model was developed based on all Phase 1 and 2 studies, and the Phase 3 Study B7841005 in participants with hemophilia A and B. The proposed dosing regimen (applied in Phase 3) is 300 mg marstacimab SC for a loading dose followed by 150 mg SC weekly (QW) maintenance dose. Meanwhile, individual participants who met protocol specified dose escalation criteria based upon breakthrough bleed were allowed to have the dose increased to 300 mg SC QW.
L1284: The PPK analysis was conducted using 2235 evaluable marstacimab concentrations in a total of 150 participants and 63 healthy participants. The summary of studies used for PPK analysis are listed in Table 94. Covariate analysis identified the covariates in Table 95 for the parameter estimates in the final model. In summary, the effect of population type (healthy versus hemophilia participants), ADA participant status (ADA positive versus ADA negative), hepatic function, race category (Asians versus non-Asians), renal impairment (normal versus mild renal impairment) and hemophilia type (hemophilia A versus hemophilia B) were estimated. Their
L1285: effect on pharmacokinetics of marstacimab was not considered significant after accounting for the weight-adjusted effect on CL.

L1295: The PK of marstacimab and TFPI were best characterized by a semi-mechanistic, two-compartment TMDD model with quasi-steady state approximation and additional nonlinear clearance of the drug in the model. Normalized weight was included as a structural covariate in the model. The final marstacimab PK model parameter estimates, and the corresponding 95% confidence intervals (CIs) are presented in Table 96.

L1322: The review team found the Applicant's TMDD PPK model adequate to describe the marstacimab serum concentration-time profiles in the observed range following the administration of marstacimab in participants with hemophilia A and B from all clinical studies. The residual plots didn't indicate any obvious bias in the parameter estimates. Therefore, the PK model is acceptable for simulating post-hoc exposure metrics, e.g., C_{avg,ss}, C_{max,ss}, C_{min,ss}, and AUC_{ss} of marstacimab for exposure-response (E-R) analyses of efficacy or safety measurements.
L1323: Covariate analysis identified body weight as a significant covariate and applied to the clearance and volume distribution in the final PPK model. In addition, other covariates such as the effect of population type (healthy versus hemophilia patients), ADA participant status (ADA positive versus ADA negative), hepatic function, race category (Asians versus Non-Asians), renal impairment (normal versus mild renal impairment) and hemophilia type (hemophilia A versus hemophilia B) were not identified as significant covariates for the marstacimab exposure after accounting for the weight-adjusted effect on CL.
L1324: Similar to marstacimab concentration goodness of fit plots, there is good agreement between observed values and population/individual predictions from the model for total TFPI predictions. Figure 34 shows a comparison of the observed total TFPI concentrations with population predictions (left panel) and individual predictions (right panel). Residuals-based diagnostic plots of CWRES and IWRES versus PRED, IPRED and time after first dose (in days) are shown in
L1325: Figure 35 for total TFPI concentrations. Similarly, the residuals-based diagnostic plots do not show any systematic bias in the model with population or individual predictions and time after the first dose.
